Q3 point 4 accepts that siblings may mutate the payload before the recorder sees it — the reverse direction is not considered anywhere, and the design's choice of eager full-property projection is what creates it.

#### capture-path-15 — record() pipeline — walk-guard escapes (async)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: A rejection created by the projection walk fires the recorder's own unhandledrejection net, so the recorder triggers itself while the app is healthy.
- **Mechanism**: A getter that returns or starts a promise (async lazy loader, fetch-on-access, a cached request handle) produces a rejection the synchronous try/catch cannot observe. The rejection surfaces as `unhandledrejection`, which `nets.unhandledRejections` (report.md:198) arms as a trigger. Q11's loop cuts are a `sealing` boolean that suppresses net-triggered re-seals DURING a seal, plus the HTTP marker header — no seal is in progress during capture, so the flag is down.
- **Effect**: The recorder seals and ships a bundle whose trigger it caused, once per cooldown window, indefinitely; the bundle's evidence describes the recorder's own walk with a stack pointing into app getters. Diagnosis effort is spent on an artefact of observation, and the loop is self-sustaining at capture rate.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q11 — the loop-protection list covers the recorder's throws and its own report POST, not rejections the projection walk creates in app code`
- **Rationale**: Q11's self-capture section (report.md:545) is explicit and closed about its three cuts; the re-entrancy path here runs through the nets, which Q7 arms, and neither question connects the walk's async side effects to the trigger surface.

#### capture-path-16 — projection — the `project()` extension point

- **Mode** *(medium)*: `project()` is handed the caller's live entry and can mutate it, making a config row able to alter the traffic the boundary is still dispatching.
- **Mechanism**: `project?: (entry: unknown) => unknown` (report.md:222) runs first in record(), on the shared dispatch turn, before sibling listeners have run. A projector written to slim a record — `delete entry.payload.blob`, an in-place sort, a normalization — mutates the object 0060 is still dispatching to other consumers. Nothing requires purity, and nothing hands the projector a defensive copy (a copy would defeat the point of projecting before serialization).
- **Effect**: The recorder alters the traffic it observes; downstream consumers see a mutated payload; the resulting app failure has no visible link to the recorder. This is the ONLY behaviour a config row may carry (report.md:219-222), in a codebase the report describes as loose-TS with heavy agent-authored churn — the likeliest place for such code to appear.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: The row's contract states only that project() may throw and that the throw is a counted projectionFailure. I6 is written about the recorder's outputs. Q3 point 4 accepts sibling-to-recorder mutation and is silent on recorder-to-sibling. The design created this surface in Q6's judged interface.

#### capture-path-17 — sequence/clock stamping — cross-bucket order

- **Mode** *(high)*: There is no total order across buckets, so the interleaving that is the recorder's sole distinct claim cannot be reconstructed.
- **Mechanism**: RD-1 sequence numbers are per bucket, and RD-3 explicitly reserves only a shared clock ORIGIN ("a single-threaded runtime needs no vector clock", report.md:551) — no global counter exists in `CapturedRecord`, `SealedBucket` or `RecorderHealth`. Cross-bucket order therefore rests entirely on wallMs/monoMs, which Firefox clamps by default (`privacy.reduceTimerPrecision`, ~1 ms at the floor), so at ~50 events/s across four buckets ties are the normal case, not the exception. Worse, stamping happens AFTER project → serialize → redact → meter → truncate (report.md:266-272), so a record's timestamp is when its projection finished: a 12 KB xstate event stamped after a ~17 µs walk can carry a later timestamp than an MQTT message that arrived after it.
- **Effect**: Q1's claim — "the last 200 transitions, the last 100 messages, and the last 20 HTTP exchanges as they interleaved" — is not deliverable. A reader gets a plausible but wrong causal order with no marker saying the order is unresolved, which is the well-formed-wrong-evidence class RD-5 exists to prevent, at bundle scope.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: RD-1/RD-2/RD-3 are the reservations that would have owned it and they deliberately rule a global order unnecessary; Q6's interface stamps seq per bucket; Q9's bundle carries per-bucket cut markers only. The stamp-after-projection ordering is a design decision made in the record() doc comment, and nothing in the report examines its effect on ordering.

#### capture-path-18 — sequence/clock stamping — wall clock

- **Mode** *(medium)*: A system clock step mid-session makes wallMs non-monotonic and inconsistent with the header's clock origin, with nothing detecting or marking it.
- **Mechanism**: `header.clockOrigin.wallMs` is captured once at construction and every record stores its own `wallMs` (report.md:374, 400-401). These devices run their OS from a RAM disk, so they boot without persisted time and take it from the network — a large NTP step mid-session is expected. After a step, record wallMs values jump (possibly backwards) while monoMs keeps running from `performance.timeOrigin`; the divergence between (wallMs − clockOrigin.wallMs) and monoMs makes the step detectable, but nothing computes or stamps it.
- **Effect**: Bundle timelines jump; backend dedup keyed on "(deviceId, trigger time-bucket, reason)" (Q16, report.md:567) and Q7's absence-based crash detection — both wall-clock-keyed and both the compensation for deaths that produce no report — mis-bucket or miss entirely. Cross-device correlation of the same incident silently fails.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: RD-2/RD-3 reserve two clocks precisely so replay can use the monotonic one, and Q9 stamps both — but every text assumes both clocks are sane. No invariant compares them, no marker records a step, and the RAM-disk premise that makes it likely is stated elsewhere in the same plan.

#### capture-path-19 — sequence/clock stamping — attach epochs

- **Mode** *(high)*: Records carry no epoch, so a bundle spanning a detach and re-attach cannot locate the capture gap, and `seq > cut.seq` may misclassify old records.
- **Mechanism**: `detach()` never discards the ring (report.md:283-285) and `attach()` supersede increments the epoch. `CapturedRecord` (report.md:399-413) has seq, clocks, kind, direction, phase, outcome, correlationId, mime/encoding, bytes, body, markers — no epoch. `SealedBucket.attach` (report.md:392) carries one `{attachedSince, detachedAt, detachReason, epoch}` — the latest. Whether seq continues or restarts across an attach is stated nowhere; if it restarts, pre-gap records collide with post-gap ones and I5's rule "seq > cut.seq ⇒ post-trigger" (report.md:396) tags stale history as aftermath.
- **Effect**: Exactly the case epochs were invented for — the worked example's boundary-recreation recovery (report.md:485-487) — yields a bundle in which the gap is invisible, the single attach block describes only the newest tap, and record ordering may be wrong. "When capture stopped is itself evidence" is asserted and not carried in the data.
- **Owner**: `partial:I5/RD-1 — epoch is stamped at bucket level and seq at record level, and no text reconciles them across an attach`
- **Rationale**: The plan pass (shortlist 14) forced attached-since markers and tap liveness into the health block, which Q6 delivers. What the design then added — supersede semantics plus a ring that survives detach — creates a two-epoch ring the record schema cannot describe.

#### capture-path-20 — tap liveness — the wire-2 self-detach predicate

- **Mode** *(high)*: The mqtt tap watches only `connection === 'ended'`, so the states in which capture actually stops leave the tap marked healthy.
- **Mechanism**: The worked example wires liveness as `if (snap.connection === 'ended') mqttTap.detach('connection-ended')` (report.md:481-483). 0060's wire-2 state set is `'idle' | 'connecting' | 'connected' | 'reconnecting' | 'degraded' | 'ended'` (0060 spike design.md:296). The states where messages stop while the app keeps running — `'degraded'`, a long `'reconnecting'`, and a tab whose boundary never connected (`'idle'`/`'connecting'`) — never reach `'ended'`, so the predicate never fires.
- **Effect**: An empty MQTT bucket reads as "attached, quiet" = "no traffic", which is the misdiagnosis Q16 (report.md:567) cites this exact machinery as dissolving for the connectionless tab, and which the three-diagnosis table (report.md:503-507) has no row for. The design's stated answer to the plan pass's highest-severity multi-tab finding is falsified by its own worked example.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q16/RecorderTap.detach — the mechanism is cited as the answer, but the predicate it uses cannot fire in the states that produce the failure it claims to dissolve`
- **Rationale**: Plan shortlist 1 forced Q16 into existence; Q16's resolution rests entirely on "the mqtt tap self-detaches on wire-2 connection-ended and its attachedSince/detachReason ride every bundle". Checking that sentence against 0060's state union is the whole of this finding.

#### capture-path-21 — drop accounting — positional loss

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Projection failures and evictions leave no in-band trace, while Q12 commits to gap markers in arrival order that no pipeline step produces.
- **Mechanism**: A projection failure never becomes a record (I4), and stamping happens after projection, so no sequence number is consumed and the surviving stream has no seq hole to read. Evictions remove records from the front. Both are aggregate counters only. Yet Q12 (report.md:551) commits that the MQTT record stream carries "explicit gap markers in arrival order — exactly the `ValidatedMessage | 'gap'` shape 0070's `RaceHarness.push` consumes". Nothing in record() emits a gap record, and `CapturedRecord` has no gap variant.
- **Effect**: A burst of projection failures — one poisoned payload shape at 50/s — is invisible in place: the reader sees a contiguous, plausible sequence while a counter elsewhere reports thousands of failures, with no way to locate them relative to the transitions that matter. The RaceHarness regression path Q12 pre-commits to cannot be fed from a real bundle.
- **Owner**: `partial:I4/Q12 — the ledger reconciles in aggregate ("a bundle accounts for every absence"), but the in-band gap marker Q12 promises has no producer in the capture pipeline`
- **Rationale**: I4 is honest about totals and RD-9 covers loss before the window via droppedBeforeWindow; mid-stream positional loss is the case neither covers, and Q12's format pre-commitment assumes it is covered.

#### capture-path-22 — eviction — starvation inside a bucket

- **Mode** *(high)*: Per-bucket ceilings close cross-bucket starvation and leave the same failure intact one level down, inside the mqtt bucket.
- **Mechanism**: Q2's answer to the plan pass's starvation finding is static per-bucket ceilings — "each pays for itself" (report.md:63). But a bucket is a TAP, not a channel: the whole MQTT stream shares mqtt's single 16 MiB / 5 000 row, so one chatty or large-payload channel evicts every other channel's context inside the bucket. `maxEntryBytes`'s default of `maxBytes/8` lets eight records consume the whole budget. Per-bucket rule sets and any per-row behaviour beyond `project()` are refused (report.md:102, 180-181), so no per-channel sub-budget is expressible. `DropCounters.evicted` merges count and byte pressure by definition — "ring pressure (count or bytes)", report.md:353 — so the operator cannot tell which cap bound, let alone which channel paid.
- **Effect**: The rare context the recorder exists to retain is still starved by redundant high-volume traffic, while the report reads as having closed the finding by construction. Retained history depth also swings ~4× with payload size (5 000 records at 2 KB vs ~256 at 64 KB) with no signal that the configured depth was never achieved.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q2 — cross-bucket starvation is closed statically; intra-bucket starvation is neither named nor bounded, and the merged eviction counter removes the evidence that would reveal it`
- **Rationale**: Plan shortlist 9 was about a binding GLOBAL envelope, and Q2's static-validation answer genuinely closes that. The design then concentrates all MQTT traffic in one row, which recreates the same dynamics inside it — a mode the design created, not one the plan pass raised.

#### capture-path-23 — seal — quarantine ride-along (capture-path adjacency)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The quarantine section is copied by reference at seal, is unprojected and unmetered, and its serialization happens off the trigger turn with none of I1's guarantees.
- **Mechanism**: Q4 (report.md:84) has the mqtt bucket copy `quarantine.entries()` into the bundle "at seal", while Q7 (report.md:515) puts assembly, redaction and serialization async off the trigger turn. The copy is therefore of references to 0060's live quarantine records — raw, schema-rejected payloads that passed no projection scrubber — held until an assembly step that may run hundreds of ms later, during which 0060 keeps evicting and refilling its own ring. These bytes are counted by no bucket budget (I3 meters only what record() stored), and the walk that serializes them runs outside record()'s guard.
- **Effect**: The one bundle section carrying unvalidated traffic can mutate between seal and assembly — the tearing I5 forbids for records, reintroduced for quarantine; it adds unbudgeted bytes to an envelope the validator already certified; and a throw in its walk (getters, cycles, BigInt in rejected payloads — likelier here than anywhere, since nothing validated these objects) has no specified counter, marker, or fallback.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q4/Q13 — Q13 makes the seal redact the ride-along and Q4 bounds it at 100 entries; nothing meters it, copies it, or extends I5's copy-not-index rule and I1's guard to it`
- **Rationale**: The plan-level pass listed "quarantine ride-along bytes belonging to no budget" among the un-acted items the surveyor should consume; Q4 designed the ride-along and Q13 covered its redaction, so the design answered part of it — and in doing so created a by-reference, unguarded path that the record pipeline's own invariants were written to forbid.

#### capture-path-24 — record() pipeline — correlation open-set

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The per-bucket open-set grows on the capture path with no bound, no meter, and no counter.
- **Mechanism**: `RecordMeta.correlationId` with `phase: 'open'` adds an id to a per-bucket open-set and `'settled'` removes it (report.md:293-299). Ids are never removed for requests that never settle — aborts (which Q5 says the two-record model exists to expose), a tap detached mid-flight, or a mutator wrap that throws before emitting the outcome record. The set is a live structure mutated by record(), outside the byte meter (which counts only serialized record bodies) and outside `DropCounters`.
- **Effect**: On a long session against a flaky backend this is the recorder's only unbounded structure, growing in the failure mode the recorder is deployed to observe. Whatever eviction rule eventually lands turns `openAtSeal` — RD-4's in-flight-at-seal evidence — from a fact into a guess, with no marker saying the set was trimmed.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q6 — named in the leave-deliberately-unsolved list ("the bounded correlation open-set needs a sizing rule before the OpenAPI contract freezes openAtSeal"), but nothing bounds it in the interim and no health counter observes it`
- **Rationale**: Q6 is honest that the sizing rule is missing, which is why this is partial rather than UNOWNED; what the honesty does not cover is that the growth happens on the capture path inside a design whose central promise is bounded-by-construction memory, and that the ledger's "no uncounted loss" claim does not extend to it.

### Seal and bundle assembly (24 modes)

#### seal-and-bundle-1 — Seal (I5 / Q7 protocol) — non-record snapshots

- **Mode** *(high)*: The seal's copy invariant covers only records, so the correlation open-set, DropCounters and health can be captured by reference and tear exactly the way I5 forbids for rings.
- **Mechanism**: I5 mandates "O(n) copy of immutable record references + a per-bucket cut marker" and nothing else. Q7 and capture()'s doc then say "snapshot DropCounters and the correlation open-set" and the header carries a whole `health: RecorderHealth` — all described as snapshots, none given a copy depth. The open-set is a live mutable Set the http tap mutates on every `phase:'settled'` record; `health()` is specified as "a cheap immutable snapshot" whose `BucketHealth.drops` is a nested object. Assembly runs async off the trigger turn, so `openAtSeal: [...openSet]` vs `openAtSeal: openSet` (and `{...bucketHealth}` leaving `drops` shared) is a one-token difference that changes what the bundle says, with no counter able to notice.
- **Effect**: A bundle can report zero in-flight correlation ids for the hang that caused the trigger (they settled between seal and assembly) and drop counters read at assembly time rather than at the cut — well-formed wrong evidence of exactly the class I5 exists to prevent, one structure over.
- **Owner**: `partial:I5 — the invariant's copy rule names only record references and the cut marker; the other three seal-time captures are prose "snapshots" with no defined copy depth`
- **Rationale**: I5 text: "Seal = O(n) copy of immutable record references + a per-bucket cut marker {seq, wallMs, monoMs}. Index snapshots are forbidden — they tear under continued capture." Q7: "then snapshot the health ledger and the correlation open-set." Nothing in the interface or Q7 states that these snapshots must be deep/eager, and Q15's "seal under load" spike is scoped to "continued capture during seal never contaminates the sealed copy" — i.e. the records, not the ledger or the open-set.

#### seal-and-bundle-2 — Seal (I5) / record path (RecordMeta.correlationId)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The correlation open-set is called bounded but has no bound, so it is an unmetered memory path and an unbounded array in the wire payload.
- **Mechanism**: RecordMeta documents "a bounded per-bucket open-set: phase 'open' adds the id, 'settled' removes it", and deliberately decouples it from ring eviction ("in-flight-at-seal is explicit even when either half was evicted"). Q6's own leave-list admits "the bounded correlation open-set needs a sizing rule before the OpenAPI contract freezes `openAtSeal`". Under loose TS with agent churn the expected defect — a custom bucket that stamps `phase:'open'` and never emits the settled half, or an app that mints a fresh correlationId per retry — grows the Set without limit. It holds strings, not records, so I3's byte meter never sees it and the config validator's Σ maxBytes arithmetic cannot price it.
- **Effect**: Unbounded main-thread growth inside the one component whose central claim is bounded-by-construction, plus an `openAtSeal` array of arbitrary length in every bundle — the recorder degrading the app on a RAM-disk device, which the failure bias forbids.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q6 — the deliberately-unsolved list names the missing sizing rule, but the interface still ships the structure and the contract field with the word "bounded" asserting what no rule provides`
- **Rationale**: Q6: "the bounded correlation open-set needs a sizing rule before the OpenAPI contract freezes openAtSeal." I3 bounds "count ≤ maxCount and bytes ≤ maxBytes" per bucket — the open-set is neither. No spike in question 15 tests open-set growth; the boundedness properties are scoped to ring bytes/counts and retention.

#### seal-and-bundle-3 — Post-trigger window (Q8 / interface trigger.postTrigger)

- **Mode** *(high)*: Nothing in the design says how records captured during the post-trigger window reach the bundle, and both readings of the silence reintroduce a defect the design already ruled out.
- **Mechanism**: I5 makes the seal a trigger-instant act (copy references, stamp the cut). Q8 then ships "one bundle whose records are partitioned by the cut markers", which requires a second collection at window close. Reading A: re-read the live ring at window close and take everything with `seq > cut.seq` — at ~50 msg/s a 2 s window is ~100 mqtt records, and any of them evicted before the window closes vanish with the bundle's counters (snapshotted at the cut) unable to show it; this is the same read-the-live-ring-later hazard I5 forbids, merely later in the timeline. Reading B: `record()` tees post-cut records into the open bundle — a second write path inside the hot loop that RecorderTap.record()'s stated sequence (project → serialize → redact → meter → truncate → stamp → evict) does not contain.
- **Effect**: Either the aftermath silently loses records with no counter (wrong evidence), or the hot capture path grows an unspecified branch inside 0060's shared dispatch turn — the latency surface the whole design is disciplined around.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q8 — Q8 designs the window, the two cuts and the one-bundle shipping rule, but never states the mechanism by which window records are collected; I5 covers only the trigger instant`
- **Rationale**: Q8: "the seal happens in two cuts: the trigger cut … and the window cut (the aftermath), shipped as one bundle whose records are partitioned by the cut markers." I5: seal is a copy at the trigger. RecorderTap.record()'s documented in-order sequence has no window branch, and Q15's seal-under-load spike names only "continued capture during seal", not capture during an open window.

#### seal-and-bundle-4 — Bundle schema (SealedBucket) vs post-trigger window (Q8)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: SealedBucket carries exactly one `cut` and counters "at cut" while Q8 ships two cuts in one bundle, so the aftermath's extent is unrepresentable and the ledger identity no longer reconciles.
- **Mechanism**: The type is `cut: {seq, wallMs, monoMs}` (singular) plus `counters: DropCounters // at cut`, with `records` documented as "seq order; seq > cut.seq ⇒ post-trigger". With a configured window the bundle contains records above `cut.seq` but carries no window-close marker — a reader cannot tell whether the aftermath ran 50 ms or the full `afterMs`, whether it ended by time or by item count, or whether the last record is the last one captured or the last one that fitted. Meanwhile `counters` frozen at the trigger cut exclude every eviction, truncation, oversized drop and projection failure that occurred during the window, while `records` includes the window's retained entries.
- **Effect**: I4's headline invariant (recorded = retained + evicted + oversized) is arithmetically false for any bundle with a non-zero window — retained exceeds what the counters imply — and the aftermath's completeness cannot be judged, which is the one thing a post-trigger window exists to give.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q8 — the two-cut design is stated in prose but the sealed schema it must serialize into has one cut and one counter block, and no question reconciles them`
- **Rationale**: Interface: `readonly cut: {…}; // I5` and `readonly counters: DropCounters; // at cut`. Q8: "two cuts… partitioned by the cut markers" (plural). I4: "The ledger reconciles… A bundle accounts for every absence; there is no uncounted loss." The default `{ms: 0}` hides this in the common path, so it will surface only when someone first configures a window.

#### seal-and-bundle-5 — Post-trigger window budget (interface trigger.postTrigger vs Q8)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The post-trigger budget is global in the interface and per-bucket in Q8, and under the global reading the noisy bucket consumes the entire aftermath before any quiet bucket records anything.
- **Mechanism**: The interface says "first of ms / maxItems, across all buckets"; Q8 says "a configurable per-bucket post-trigger window — {afterMs, afterCount}, whichever fills first" — different scope and different field names (`ms`/`maxItems` vs `afterMs`/`afterCount`), so the contradiction is textual, not interpretive. Under the interface's global `maxItems`, at ~50 msg/s the mqtt bucket alone exhausts a 100-item budget in ~2 s while xstate transitions and log records arrive at a fraction of that rate; the window closes with the aftermath consisting almost entirely of MQTT traffic.
- **Effect**: The aftermath systematically excludes the sparse buckets whose entries are the rare context — the exact starvation argument Q2 used to reject a runtime cross-bucket envelope, recurring at the window where Q2's per-bucket answer was explicitly refused.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q8 — Q8 answers per-bucket while the interface artifact (Q6) refuses per-bucket windows and defines a global budget; neither text notices the other, and Q2's starvation reasoning is not applied here`
- **Rationale**: Interface: "Trigger discipline — global; per-bucket windows refused… first of ms / maxItems, across all buckets." Q8: "a configurable per-bucket post-trigger window — {afterMs, afterCount}". Q2 on the analogous choice: "cross-bucket evict-oldest deterministically starves quiet buckets (xstate, logs)… With per-bucket ceilings… each pays for itself."

#### seal-and-bundle-6 — Trigger protocol (Q7 / capture() receipt)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Coalesce and suppress both apply to every trigger inside an open window, and the interface never rules which wins — so an implementation that checks cooldown first turns every coalesce into a silent suppress.
- **Mechanism**: capture()'s doc lists both rules without ordering: "'suppressed' inside cooldownMs of a seal; 'coalesced' while a post-trigger window holds a bundle open." The trigger cut *is* a seal, so from the trigger instant onward the recorder is simultaneously in cooldown and (if configured) in an open window. Q8's validator rule — "a window longer than the cooldown is rejected as configuration error" — makes the window a strict subset of the cooldown, so the two predicates overlap on the window's entire duration, always. Only Q7's prose ("A trigger during an in-flight post-trigger window coalesces") breaks the tie, and it is not in the invariant list or the type.
- **Effect**: Caller-visible divergence (a receipt with a bundleId versus one without), the second trigger's reason never appended to the bundle it belongs to, and a health block that shows the storm under `suppressed` rather than `coalesced` — a silently different bundle from the one the design describes, produced by code that reads as correct.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q7 — Q7's prose rules coalescing, but the interface's own receipt documentation lists both conditions with no precedence and no invariant covers trigger-state ordering`
- **Rationale**: Interface capture(): "'suppressed' inside cooldownMs of a seal; 'coalesced' while a post-trigger window holds a bundle open. Both are counted." Q8: window ≤ cooldown enforced by the validator. Q7: "A trigger during an in-flight post-trigger window coalesces into the open window." Under loose TS with agent churn, the ordering of two `if` branches is precisely the kind of detail that drifts.

#### seal-and-bundle-7 — Trigger protocol (Q7) / BundleHeader.trigger

- **Mode** *(medium)*: A coalesced trigger contributes only its reason string; its error object, parsed stack and instant are discarded.
- **Mechanism**: `BundleHeader.trigger` is `{reason, coalescedReasons: readonly string[], error?}` — one error slot, and a bare string array for everything that coalesced. Q7 confirms it: "the reason is appended to that bundle's header." A `capture('pump-controller-stuck', { error })` that coalesces into a window opened by an earlier, possibly benign, explicit trigger loses its `CaptureOptions.error`, its tracekit-parsed stack, and the wall/mono instant at which it fired.
- **Effect**: The bundle attributes the incident to whichever trigger happened to fire first and drops the stack of the one that may actually be the fault — a silent evidence loss with no marker and no counter, in the header that a reader looks at first.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q7 — Q7 rules coalescing and names the reason append; neither Q7 nor the header schema carries per-coalesced-trigger error/stack/timestamp`
- **Rationale**: Interface: `readonly coalescedReasons: readonly string[]` and a single `readonly error?: {message, stack?, markers?}`. Q7: "(`{outcome: 'coalesced'}`; the reason is appended to that bundle's header)". Q8 rejected the supplement design partly to avoid "doubl[ing] the identity bookkeeping", so the coalesced trigger has nowhere else to land.

#### seal-and-bundle-8 — Bundle schema (CapturedRecord / SealedBucket.attach) vs attach-epoch supersede

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Records carry no attach epoch while a single `epoch` is stamped per sealed bucket, so a bundle spanning a supersede cannot attribute records to a tap generation — and the `seq > cut.seq` rule rests on unstated seq continuity across epochs.
- **Mechanism**: attach() "supersedes the first (old tap marked detached 'superseded', epoch++)", detach "never discards evidence: the ring, its records, and the death marker remain", and the worked example makes re-attach the standard boundary-recovery move ("recreate the boundary, then recorder.attach('mqtt') again — epoch++… the old ring's records remain until eviction"). So a bundle whose ring spans a boundary recreation is the expected case. `CapturedRecord` has `seq` and clocks but no epoch; `SealedBucket.attach` has one scalar `epoch`. Nothing states whether `seq` is ring-owned (continuous across epochs, as RD-1's "ring-assigned at push" hints) or tap-owned (restarting per attach) — and if it restarts, `seq > cut.seq` misclassifies post-supersede records as pre-trigger history and RD-1's ordering guarantee collapses on duplicate seq values.
- **Effect**: Either records from a dead tap generation are silently presented as belonging to the current one (the boundary-recovery bundle reads as continuous history across a gap), or the post-trigger partition inverts — both wrong evidence in the recovery scenario the design deliberately supports.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q6 — the leave-list admits "post-trigger records are identified only by seq > cut.seq (a consumer rule, no per-record flag)" but never meets that rule with the attach-epoch supersede the same interface makes routine; no text fixes seq's scope`
- **Rationale**: Interface attach() and RecorderTap.detach() as quoted; `SealedBucket.attach: {…, epoch: number}`; `CapturedRecord` field list contains no epoch. RD-1 (Q12): "per-bucket monotonic sequence numbers (ring-assigned at push)" — bucket-scoped by that reading, but the epoch lives on the tap and the interface never says the two are independent.

#### seal-and-bundle-9 — Bundle schema (DropCounters / BucketHealth) vs I4

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The flagship ledger invariant cannot be checked from a bundle, because no field carries `recorded`.
- **Mechanism**: I4 states `recorded = retained + evicted + oversized`. A bundle carries `counters: DropCounters` (evicted, truncated, oversized, projectionFailures), `BucketHealth.count` (retained) and `BucketHealth.seq` ("last stamped sequence number") — but no `recorded`. `seq` is the only candidate proxy and its relationship to `recorded` is unstated: oversized entries and projection failures never become records, so whether they consume a sequence number decides whether `seq` equals `recorded`, `recorded − oversized`, or something else. With no independent `recorded`, the identity is satisfied by construction and falsifies nothing.
- **Effect**: The bundle's central honesty claim — "a bundle accounts for every absence; there is no uncounted loss" — is unverifiable by the consumer and by the backend, so a counting bug in the owned ring ships as a plausible-looking ledger.
- **Owner**: `partial:I4 — the invariant is stated over a quantity the bundle does not carry; Q15's property tests can check it in-process but no field lets a reader check a field bundle`
- **Rationale**: I4 as quoted; the `DropCounters` and `BucketHealth` field lists as declared. Q15's boundedness lane tests "every eviction/truncation/projection-failure is counted" in a harness, which is a different check from reconciling a delivered bundle.

#### seal-and-bundle-10 — Quarantine ride-along (Q4 → Q9 bundle section)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The ride-along copies 0060's live quarantine entries at seal and serializes them later, so the one path that captures by reference is the path holding the least trustworthy objects.
- **Mechanism**: Q4: "at seal, the bucket copies `quarantine.entries()` into the bundle as a distinct section rather than recording quarantined traffic continuously." Those entries are the boundary's raw rejects — the plan's own grounding found the quarantined Ajv evidence is a shallow copy whose `ErrorObject`s Ajv reuses, with no freeze/clone specified anywhere in 0060. I2 scopes serialize-at-capture to `record()` ("When record() returns, the recorder holds no reference to the caller's entry"), and the ride-along never passes through `record()`. Serialization therefore happens at assembly — async, after Ajv has reused the objects and after any app code holding the raw payloads has mutated them.
- **Effect**: Quarantine evidence in the bundle can differ from what was quarantined (mutated `ErrorObject`s, mutated payloads), and until assembly the sealed section pins whatever graph those raw objects reference — mutation-unsafe, graph-pinning capture, the exact defect the report used to eliminate Sentry's breadcrumb ring.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q4 — Q4 designs the copy and defends it as policy-free, but no invariant covers the ride-along's serialization point, its walk guard, or its reference lifetime; I2's wording excludes it structurally`
- **Rationale**: Q4 as quoted; I2 as quoted (scoped to record()); Survey/Q3: "wire-1 payloads and telemetry errors are shared live objects (Ajv reuses `ErrorObject`s; no freeze/clone is specified anywhere in 0060)". The rejection of Sentry rests on the same property: "Breadcrumbs are stored by reference… Pre-trigger history in Sentry's ring is therefore exactly the mutation-unsafe, graph-pinning capture the charter sweep ruled out for every tap."

#### seal-and-bundle-11 — Quarantine ride-along vs I3 envelope arithmetic

- **Mode** *(high)*: Ride-along bytes are metered by nothing and budgeted by nothing, so the bundle can exceed the envelope and the endpoint ceiling by an amount the static config validator structurally cannot see.
- **Mechanism**: I3 enforces the envelope as `Σ maxBytes + header allowance ≤ envelope`, checked once at startup. The quarantine ring is 0060's: count-bounded at 100, never byte-bounded, holding raw invalid payloads of unbounded individual size, none of which passed the recorder's meter (which by I3 runs "once at capture"). The ride-along is "its own section" in the bundle, not a bucket, so it has no `maxBytes` row; the only slack term is an unsized "header allowance". 100 rejects of a large-payload channel adds unmetered megabytes at assembly time.
- **Effect**: A bundle materially larger than the envelope the whole design is denominated in, arriving at an endpoint whose ceiling is intake item c — on the device class where transient memory is the stated hazard; the static-validation answer to the envelope silently stops being an answer.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — the plan-level pass raised this exact mode and it appears in the register's "not acted on" list ("quarantine ride-along bytes belong to no budget"); the design adds the ride-along to the bundle without adding a term for it anywhere`
- **Rationale**: I3: "The global 10–50 MB envelope is enforced STATICALLY by the config validator (Σ maxBytes + header allowance ≤ envelope), never by cross-bucket eviction." Q4 adds the section; Q9 says only "The quarantine ride-along is its own section with the same record discipline"; plan-fmea line 136-139 lists it among findings the surveyor should read that were not acted on.

#### seal-and-bundle-12 — Quarantine ride-along vs RD-1/RD-2/RD-3 record discipline

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Ride-along entries have no recorder-domain clock or sequence, so "the same record discipline" is either fabricated or unsatisfiable against the contract.
- **Mechanism**: `CapturedRecord` requires `seq`, `wallMs`, and `monoMs` ("offset from header.clockOrigin"). Quarantine entries were placed in 0060's ring at their own rejection time and the recorder first sees them at seal. Stamping them with the seal's clock misdates them by up to the ring's whole retention window; passing through 0060's own stamps (if any exist) mixes clock domains inside one bundle against RD-3's single shared origin; omitting them produces records that fail the generated validator — which, per Q10, is only run at build/test time, so the failure surfaces as a backend 400 in the field.
- **Effect**: Quarantined evidence is either misordered against every other bucket (defeating the cross-bucket interleaving that is the recorder's one distinct claim, per Q1) or makes the bundle contract-invalid in production.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — the plan-level pass raised timestamp provenance for trigger-time-read evidence; the design's answer ("its own section with the same record discipline") asserts conformance without supplying the clock or the sequence`
- **Rationale**: `CapturedRecord` field list; RD-2/RD-3 in Q12 ("one shared clock domain… the origin is stamped once in the envelope"); Q9: "The quarantine ride-along is its own section with the same record discipline." Q4 says the recorder "inherits the ring's own bound (100) and touches no policy" — which is exactly why it cannot have stamped the entries.

#### seal-and-bundle-13 — Seal (I1/I4) — trigger header and ride-along projection

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Failures during the seal's own projection work have no ledger species, so the design's "no uncounted loss" rule has a hole at the seal.
- **Mechanism**: `DropCounters` has four species and lives per bucket. The seal projects things that belong to no bucket: `CaptureOptions.error` (an `unknown` from app code — reading `.stack`/`.message` can invoke a getter that throws, and a custom Error subclass's `toJSON` runs during assembly), `CaptureOptions.context` (arbitrary app object), and the quarantine ride-along (unvalidated payloads, arbitrary getters/proxies). I1 guarantees `capture()` does not throw outward, but nothing says what a swallowed seal-side failure increments; there is no `headerFailures`, no quarantine counter, and no bucket to charge.
- **Effect**: A bundle can arrive with a missing stack, a missing context block, or a missing quarantine section and nothing in it says so — the well-formed-wrong-evidence class, produced by the very machinery the ledger exists to make honest.
- **Owner**: `partial:I4 — the ledger is defined per bucket over four species, all originating in `record()`; I1 promises no outward throw at `capture()` but names no counter for what it absorbs`
- **Rationale**: I1: "every internal failure — a projection walk that throws in app getters/proxies/toJSON included — is a COUNTED drop", with `DropCounters` (evicted/truncated/oversized/projectionFailures) as the only counters and `BucketHealth` as their only home. Q3's walk guard is written for `record()` ("never a throw into the tap"), and Q7's protocol lists the header stamp without a guard.

#### seal-and-bundle-14 — Seal turn budget (Q7 vs Q13)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Q7 puts seal-side redaction in the async phase while Q13 says the seal applies it, and under Q13's reading an unbounded redaction walk runs inside the synchronous trigger turn on the degraded device.
- **Mechanism**: Q7: "Everything after — assembly, redaction of the trigger header itself (question 13), serialization, optional compression, delivery — is async off the trigger turn." Q13: "the seal applies the same rules to the envelope's own free text — the trigger's error message and stack…, the config snapshot…, and the quarantine ride-along… — before assembly." The ride-along is up to 100 raw payloads of unbounded size; the conservative default rule set is structural-skeleton redaction, i.e. a full walk of every leaf. Q11's budget claim assumes the other reading: "the seal path allocates only pointer copies plus the per-bucket chunk in flight."
- **Effect**: Under the Q13 reading, `capture()` — called from `window.onerror` at the worst moment — blocks the main thread for an unbounded walk over the largest unvalidated payloads in the system; under the Q7 reading, unredacted free text and raw rejects sit in a live object graph for the duration of async assembly. The two readings have different app-degradation and different PII profiles, and both are in the report.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q13 — Q13 assigns the work to the seal, Q7 assigns it to the async phase, and Q11's spike budget is written as if only pointer copies happen synchronously; no text reconciles them`
- **Rationale**: Q7, Q13 and Q11 as quoted. The spike list's "seal under load" lane tests contamination of the sealed copy, not the seal turn's duration with a ride-along present.

#### seal-and-bundle-15 — Bundle assembly (Q9 / I9 worker path)

- **Mode** *(high)*: "Streams per bucket into the worker" still means building a bucket-sized JSON string on the main thread — 16 MiB for the worked mqtt row — and it is a second full serialization of data already serialized once at capture.
- **Mechanism**: The worked config gives mqtt `maxBytes: 16 * MiB`. A per-bucket chunk is therefore a `JSON.stringify` over ~5,000 records whose `body` fields are already JSON strings: each body must be re-escaped as a string literal, so assembly copies and expands every byte the ring holds. Peak for that one chunk is the UTF-16 string (~32 MB) plus its `TextEncoder` output (~16 MB) before the buffer can be transferred, on top of the still-live rings. The claim being defended — "capping the peak at roughly envelope + largest-bucket rather than 2–3× envelope" — counts the largest bucket once, not three times, and treats the assembly pass as free.
- **Effect**: A multi-hundred-millisecond synchronous main-thread block plus a ~48 MB allocation at the exact moment the device is already degraded — the renderer-OOM case that Q7 classifies as a death producing no report at all, caused by the collection step.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — Q9 fixes the design lever (per-bucket chunks, transferables) and defers the number to the spike, but its stated peak arithmetic omits the string→bytes doubling and the re-serialization cost, so the spike as scoped may measure the wrong ceiling`
- **Rationale**: Q9: "assembly streams per bucket into the worker (bucket-sized chunks, transferable buffers)… capping the peak at roughly envelope + largest-bucket." Q11: "the seal path allocates only pointer copies plus the per-bucket chunk in flight." The worked example's `mqtt: { maxBytes: 16 * MiB }` sets the chunk size. Q15's spike list has no assembly-cost lane — its memory lane is boundedness-as-retention on the ring.

#### seal-and-bundle-16 — Bundle assembly vs I3 metering (wire bytes)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Delivered bytes exceed metered bytes by content-proportional factors the envelope arithmetic models as a per-record constant.
- **Mechanism**: Two expansions are proportional to content, not to `maxCount`. (1) Every record `body` is a JSON string embedded in JSON, so every quote, backslash and control character doubles and every non-ASCII character can become a `\uXXXX` escape — 6 bytes for 2 metered UTF-8 bytes on some payloads. (2) `markers?: readonly LossMarker[]` is unbounded per record, and Q13's declared conservative default is structural-skeleton redaction ("keys kept, leaf values redacted") with RD-10 stamping `{redacted, ruleId, at}` on every application — so a 2 KB record with 200 leaves can carry more marker bytes than body bytes. Q2 declares the overhead model: "the ring's per-entry fixed overhead (sequence number, clocks, byte length, marker flags — tens of bytes) is documented as a stated constant multiplied by maxCount."
- **Effect**: A recorder configured to 32 MiB of buckets ships a payload materially larger, against an endpoint ceiling (intake item c) and a device envelope the static validator believes it has enforced — silently, since the meter that reports `bytes` in the health block is measuring the smaller quantity.
- **Owner**: `partial:I3/Q2 — the meter is honest about UTF-16 heap drift but treats index/metadata overhead as a constant × maxCount, which is false for marker arrays and says nothing about escape expansion at assembly`
- **Rationale**: Q2's overhead paragraph as quoted; `CapturedRecord.markers` and the `LossMarker` union as declared; Q13's structural-skeleton default and RD-10's per-application stamping. I3 meters "serialized UTF-8… once at capture" — i.e. the body, before it becomes a JSON string literal inside a larger document.

#### seal-and-bundle-17 — Seal retention (I5 "O(n) copy" vs delivery horizon)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The sealed bundle's cheap pointer copy becomes an exclusive retention of a full envelope once the rings turn over, so memory is doubled or tripled for the whole retry horizon rather than spiked transiently.
- **Mechanism**: I5's copy shares strings with the ring, which is why it is cheap to make. But at ~50 msg/s the mqtt ring turns over completely in seconds; from then on the sealed bundle is the only holder of those strings. Q10 keeps one bundle in flight plus one pending, and bounds retries at "default 8 attempts / ~5 minutes horizon". So the steady state during an outage is live rings (up to one envelope, refilling) + two sealed bundles (up to two envelopes) ≈ 3× envelope, i.e. 30–150 MB held for minutes on a RAM-disk device — and Q9's peak analysis treats seal memory as transient co-residency at the trigger instant.
- **Effect**: Sustained memory pressure at exactly the multiple Q10's own bounded-then-drop reasoning was designed to avoid ("the pinned bundle is 10–50 MB of RAM on a RAM-disk device") — understated by 2–3× because the sealed copies were priced as pointers.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — Q9 budgets peak memory at the seal instant and Q10 prices one pinned bundle; neither prices the sealed-copy retention across the retry horizon with two slots occupied and the rings refilling underneath`
- **Rationale**: I5: "O(n) copy of immutable record references". Q10: "one bundle in flight at a time plus at most one pending" and "default 8 attempts / ~5 minutes horizon". Q9: "The transient co-residency — sealed record copies (pointer copies; the strings are shared), the assembled body string, and its worker copy — is bounded by construction." The word "transient" is doing the work the timeline does not support.

#### seal-and-bundle-18 — Bundle assembly on the retry path (Q9 × Q10)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Nothing says the assembled bytes are cached, so each of up to 8 retry attempts can repeat the full assembly, re-escaping and re-encoding the envelope.
- **Mechanism**: The transport receives a `SealedBundle` object, not bytes: `transport: (bundle: SealedBundle) => void | Promise<void>`. Retry/backoff live behind that seam. If the transport re-derives the wire body per attempt (the natural shape when the retry loop wraps the POST), the 16 MiB chunk assembly, the escape expansion, the encoding and the gzip run 8 times over ~5 minutes on the degraded device. Q9 also has the transport adapter read 0070's `IngressStats` "at assembly time and stamp them with their own read clock" — so successive attempts of the same `bundleId` carry different environment blocks, while the backend "upserts on (deviceId, pageIncarnationId, bundleId)" and keeps one arbitrarily.
- **Effect**: Repeated multi-tens-of-MB allocations and main-thread work throughout an outage, and a stored bundle whose environment block came from an arbitrary attempt rather than from the incident.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — Q9 designs assembly for one pass, Q10 designs retries behind the seam, and no text says the assembled bytes are produced once and reused, or that IngressStats are read once`
- **Rationale**: Interface `transport` signature and its doc ("Retry/backoff/one-at-a-time, worker-side gzip, and the wire format live behind it"); Q9's IngressStats paragraph; Q10's dedup rule. Because assembly is behind the same seam as retry, the recorder cannot enforce single-assembly even if the report intended it.

#### seal-and-bundle-19 — Transport seam (I6) × failure nets (Q11)

- **Mode** *(high)*: A transport that throws synchronously or rejects turns the recorder into a self-driven bundle loop, because I6's "its errors are its own to absorb" is an instruction to app code, not a boundary the recorder holds.
- **Mechanism**: `transport` is app-supplied and typed `void | Promise<void>`; it is invoked behind the seam, off the trigger turn. A synchronous throw there (a bad Worker URL, a `JSON.stringify` over a BigInt in a custom bucket, any of the churn-prone assembly code) surfaces as an uncaught exception → `window.onerror`, which is a recorder net → `capture()` → seal → delivery → transport → throws again. An unhandled rejection takes the same route via the `unhandledrejection` net. Q11's loop cuts do not reach this: the HTTP-wrap filter covers the recorder's own POST, and the `sealing` boolean "suppresses net-triggered re-seals" only "during an active seal" — delivery happens long after the seal turn ends. The only limiter left is `cooldownMs`, so the steady state is one full seal-and-assemble cycle every 30 s, forever.
- **Effect**: The recorder becomes the incident: a permanent cycle of envelope-sized assemblies and failed POSTs on a degraded device, driven by its own delivery failure — the outcome the failure bias explicitly forbids.
- **Owner**: `partial:I6 — I6 states the obligation on the transport ("Its errors are its own to absorb") without any recorder-side mechanism (no try/catch, no `.catch()`, no post-delivery re-entrancy suppression) and I1's never-throw list covers the inbound entry points only`
- **Rationale**: I6 and the `transport` doc as quoted; I1 lists "record(), attach(), detach(), capture() and health()" — transport is an outbound call and is not covered. Q11: "the nets ignore errors whose stack tops in recorder frames during an active seal (re-entrancy flag…: a `sealing` boolean suppresses net-triggered re-seals, counted)" — scoped to the seal turn.

#### seal-and-bundle-20 — Delivery policy (Q10) vs the transport seam (I9/Q6)

- **Mode** *(high)*: Every delivery guarantee the report states — one in flight plus one pending, evict-older-pending, 8 bounded attempts, a `deliveryFailed` counter riding the next bundle — lives inside app-supplied code the recorder cannot enforce, and the counter has no field to ride in.
- **Mechanism**: Q10 ends its own rules with "(All of this lives behind question 6's `transport` seam — it is delivery policy, invisible to the capture surface.)" The recorder hands over a `SealedBundle` and gets `void | Promise<void>` back. It therefore cannot cap how many bundles the transport retains (a transport that queues pins N × envelope, defeating I3 outside the ring), cannot know an attempt failed, and cannot count anything. And `DropCounters` has exactly four species with no `deliveryFailed`, while `RecorderHealth` has `buckets`, `triggers`, `configErrors` and `unknownBuckets` — no delivery block at all. Q9's loss marker also promises to carry drop counters plus `sealedButUndelivered`, which the recorder likewise cannot know.
- **Effect**: The report's delivery section describes behaviour the recommended interface makes unobservable and unenforceable; in the field, evidence loss during delivery goes uncounted (contradicting I4's "no uncounted loss") and unbounded bundle retention by a churn-authored transport is outside every bound the design claims.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q10 — Q10 rules the policy and then assigns it behind a seam whose signature carries neither the outcome back nor a slot for its counter; the interface's health and DropCounters types contradict the prose`
- **Rationale**: Q10 as quoted, including "the drop increments `deliveryFailed`, and the counter rides the next bundle and the last-gasp loss marker". `DropCounters` and `RecorderHealth` field lists as declared. I9: "bundling, compression (worker-side), and delivery run behind the seam" — so this is deliberate, and the counters were simply never reconciled with it.

#### seal-and-bundle-21 — Bundle header (Q9 configDigest) vs RedactionRules

- **Mode** *(medium)*: `RedactionRules.patterns` are `RegExp` objects, which vanish under JSON serialization, so the config digest cannot distinguish two fleets with different pattern rules.
- **Mechanism**: `RedactionRules { keys?: string[]; paths?: string[]; patterns?: RegExp[] }`. The header carries "a config digest plus the validation residue rather than the raw config snapshot — the digest satisfies RD-7's attribution need". Any digest computed over a JSON projection of the config renders each RegExp as `{}` (RegExp has no enumerable own properties and no `toJSON`), so a config with `[/ssn-\d+/]`, one with `[/token=.*/]`, and one with `patterns: []` all digest identically unless the digest routine special-cases `RegExp.prototype.source`/`flags` — which nothing in the design says.
- **Effect**: The one header field meant to attribute a bundle's redaction posture is blind to the rule class most likely to differ between deployments; a reader of a heavily redacted bundle cannot tell which rules produced it, and a fleet-wide misconfiguration of patterns is invisible in the reports it thinned.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — Q9 introduces the digest to dissolve the secrets-in-config hazard and RD-7 wants attribution, but no text defines what the digest is computed over or how non-JSON config values are folded in`
- **Rationale**: `RedactionRules` as declared; Q9: "a config digest plus the validation residue rather than the raw config snapshot — the digest satisfies RD-7's attribution need (the resolvable config lives in deployment)." Q13 makes the rule set the gating deliverable pending intake item b, which is what makes attribution load-bearing.

#### seal-and-bundle-22 — Bundle identity (Q9) — id minting primitive

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The identity fields the whole dedup and absence-detection story rests on have no named primitive, and the obvious ones (`crypto.randomUUID`, `crypto.subtle`) are secure-context-only.
- **Mechanism**: Q9 specifies `pageIncarnationId` as "a UUID minted once per page load" and the worked example shows a ULID-shaped `bundleId` (`b_01J…`), naming no API for either. `crypto.randomUUID()` and `crypto.subtle` (the only built-in hash, and async, for a digest) are exposed only in secure contexts — on an `http://` origin that is not localhost they are `undefined`. This fleet's browser-compat row floor-checks TextEncoder, CompressionStream, sendBeacon and BroadcastChannel; `crypto.*` and the origin's scheme appear nowhere in the report or the intake list.
- **Effect**: On a plain-http deployment the id minting throws inside `createFlightRecorder`, which I8 forbids from throwing — so either the recorder comes up disabled at boot on the whole fleet, or ids silently fall back to something unspecified, and backend dedup on `(deviceId, pageIncarnationId, bundleId)` degrades on identical devices.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — Q9 names the identity fields and the dedup key, and the rubric's browser-compatibility row floor-checks four other primitives; nothing names the id/digest primitive or the secure-context precondition, and no intake item asks the origin's scheme`
- **Rationale**: Q9's identity paragraph as quoted; the worked receipt `{ outcome: 'sealed', bundleId: 'b_01J…' }`; rubric row: "strong — every primitive floor-checked this survey (TextEncoder, CompressionStream FF113+, sendBeacon FF31+, BroadcastChannel FF38+)". I8: "createFlightRecorder NEVER throws".

#### seal-and-bundle-23 — Bundle validity vs the OpenAPI contract (Q9/Q10)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: A bundle that violates the vendored contract is discovered only by the backend, and the delivery loop treats that permanent rejection like a transient outage.
- **Mechanism**: Q10 deliberately skips runtime validation of the outbound POST ("runtime validation of an outbound diagnostic POST adds cost in exactly the degraded moment and is explicitly skipped"), leaving 0010's generated validators to run at build/test time only. The bundle's most contract-hostile parts are runtime-shaped: `unknownBuckets` and `health.buckets` are `Record<string, …>` keyed by app-supplied strings, app-declared buckets carry arbitrary `schemaId`/`schemaVersion`, the ride-along section may lack required record fields (see the clock-provenance mode), and `markers` is an undiscriminated union of three literal-flag shapes. Q10's retry policy makes no distinction between a 4xx and a 5xx: "default 8 attempts / ~5 minutes horizon".
- **Effect**: Eight POSTs of a tens-of-MB body over five minutes for a bundle that can never be accepted — bandwidth and main-thread work spent on a permanent failure, ending in a `deliveryFailed` counter that (per the delivery-seam mode) has nowhere to be recorded.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q10 — Q10 argues the runtime-validation exemption and rules bounded retries, but never distinguishes permanent from transient failure, and Q15's "bundle validity" spike lane covers only bundles the test lane can construct`
- **Rationale**: Q10 as quoted; Q15: "Bundle validity: every sealed bundle validates against the vendored OpenAPI schema via 0010's generated validators in the test lane" — a lane that sees declared buckets and synthetic data, not field-shaped `unknownBuckets` keys or ride-along sections.

#### seal-and-bundle-24 — Last-gasp loss marker (Q9) × post-trigger window (Q8)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The loss marker's arming condition misses the window-open state and its payload names only one undelivered bundle, so the two most likely dismissal-time losses are reported as silence.
- **Mechanism**: Q9 arms the beacon "only when a sealed bundle is in flight or pending", carrying `sealedButUndelivered: bundleId` (singular). A bundle held open in a post-trigger window is neither in flight nor pending — it has been cut but not assembled — so a tab dismissed during a configured window emits nothing, and the trigger that caused it leaves no trace anywhere. Separately, Q10 allows two undelivered bundles at once (one in flight, one pending); the marker's single `bundleId` can name only one of them.
- **Effect**: The design's stated compensation for the no-durable-parking ruling — "it converts 'bundle never arrived' from silence into a positively-reported loss" — has two holes in exactly the states the design itself creates, so absence-based detection at the backend sees nothing to distinguish a lost bundle from a device that was quiet.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — Q9 defines the marker's trigger condition and payload against a two-state model (in flight, pending) that Q8's window and Q10's two slots both exceed`
- **Rationale**: Q9: "`{identity, trigger reason, per-bucket drop counters, sealedButUndelivered: bundleId}`… sent on `visibilitychange`-hidden (paired with `pagehide`…) only when a sealed bundle is in flight or pending." Q8: the window "delays delivery by at most afterMs". Q10: "one bundle in flight at a time plus at most one pending".

### Triggers and failure nets (25 modes)

#### triggers-and-nets-1 — Q7 nets — arming primitive; RecorderConfig.nets

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The design never states whether a net is armed by assigning `window.onerror` or by `addEventListener('error', …)`, and the two arms see different error populations.
- **Mechanism**: Q7 says only that the nets "call any previous handler" (call-through is property-assignment semantics — addEventListener listeners chain automatically and have no "previous handler" to call), while the pre-arm stub is described as "register[ing] `error`/`unhandledrejection` listeners" (addEventListener semantics). The two primitives differ materially: `window.onerror` never fires for resource-load failures, `addEventListener('error', fn)` without capture also misses them (they do not bubble), and only `addEventListener('error', fn, true)` sees them — as bare `Event`s with no `error` object. The design picks neither, so the implementer's choice silently decides whether a failed chunk/script/image load is a trigger at all.
- **Effect**: Either the recorder is blind to the boot-critical chunk-404 class (the failure the pre-arm stub exists for), or every 404'd image becomes a full multi-MB trigger with an empty error block. Both readings are conformant with the report as written, so the fleet's behaviour is decided by whoever writes the ~30 lines, not by the design.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q7 — the nets are named and chaining is ruled, but the arming primitive (and therefore the observed error population) is never fixed; the spike list defers "net arming/chaining" to 0120's harness, which does not exist`
- **Rationale**: report.md:196-198 (`nets?: { windowErrors?: boolean; unhandledRejections?: boolean }`, "the nets call any previous handler") and report.md:513-517 contain no occurrence of `addEventListener`, `onerror =`, or capture-phase. Line 630 pushes "net arming/chaining" into a deferred real-browser lane, so nothing in the report or its verification plan closes the choice.

#### triggers-and-nets-2 — Q7 nets — chaining semantics; I6 (observation only)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The net's return value / `preventDefault` behaviour is unspecified, so a chained net can suppress default error reporting the app or an incumbent relies on — an observation-only tool changing handling behaviour.
- **Mechanism**: `window.onerror` returning `true` cancels the default action (the browser's console reporting); for an `error`/`unhandledrejection` listener the equivalent is `event.preventDefault()`, which silences the console's unhandled-rejection warning. The design's only ruling is "call-through to prior handlers" (report.md:616). If the net propagates a truthy return from an incumbent, or returns truthy itself, console reporting dies fleet-wide; if it returns undefined after an incumbent returned true, it *undoes* the incumbent's suppression.
- **Effect**: The recorder alters error-handling behaviour in both directions while I6 promises it "classifies nothing, participates in no transaction". Silenced console output is also the developer-visible symptom least likely to be attributed to the recorder.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — no report text, invariant, or intake item addresses the return value of the nets or `preventDefault``
- **Rationale**: Grep of report.md finds no `preventDefault` and no discussion of return values; Q7's chaining sentence and assumption g (report.md:616) speak only of "call-through to prior handlers". I1 constrains throws outward, not side effects on the event.

#### triggers-and-nets-3 — Q7 nets — chaining call order and isolation of the incumbent handler

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The order of `capture()` versus the call-through, and containment of a throwing incumbent, are both unspecified — a broken incumbent can silently disarm the recorder's capture or amplify into an error-in-error-handler loop.
- **Mechanism**: If the net calls the prior handler first and that handler throws (a stale analytics snippet, an incumbent reporter whose own transport is dead), the recorder's `capture()` is never reached — the recorder is dead for exactly the failures the incumbent also cannot report. If it calls capture() first and then calls through unwrapped, the incumbent's throw surfaces from inside a global error handler and is itself reported as an error, re-entering the net; the cooldown bounds seals but not the re-entry or the repeated call-through.
- **Effect**: Either the net is silently inert whenever the incumbent is broken, or the recorder converts a broken incumbent into a CPU-burning trigger loop whose only trace is an inflated `triggers.suppressed`.
- **Owner**: `partial:I1 — I1 enumerates record/attach/detach/capture/health as no-throw; a net's call-through executes third-party code outside that list, and Q11's per-tap try/catch doctrine is written for taps, not nets`
- **Rationale**: I1 at report.md:117-119 lists the five seam members. Q11's containment ruling (report.md:544) is "every tap therefore wraps its entire body in its own try/catch" with a per-tap `tapError` counter — nets are not taps and have no counter; no text orders capture() relative to the call-through.

#### triggers-and-nets-4 — Interface — RecorderHealth / BundleHeader; net liveness

- **Mode** *(high)*: Nothing anywhere records whether a net is still armed; a later `window.onerror` assignment by any other script disarms the recorder silently and permanently.
- **Mechanism**: Chaining preserves handlers present at arm time only. Any script loading later — an SDK, an analytics snippet, an agent-authored module doing `window.onerror = …` — replaces the recorder's net. `RecorderHealth` carries `enabled`, `configErrors`, `buckets`, `triggers`, `unknownBuckets` and no net state; `BundleHeader` embeds that same health block, so no bundle can report which nets were armed at seal. The design gave *taps* a full liveness apparatus — `attachedSince`, `detachedAt`, `detachReason`, `attachEpoch`, and self-observed `detach(reason)` — and gave nets none, despite I7 declaring nets peers of taps on the same surface.
- **Effect**: A disarmed net is indistinguishable from a quiet one: the fleet can run an entire release with no window-error capture while every bundle looks healthy. The absence-based backend detection of Q7 cannot distinguish it either, because bundles keep arriving from explicit triggers.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — the plan flagged the post-install clobbering direction as unowned; the design added no net-state field, no periodic re-arm check, and no health entry`
- **Rationale**: RecorderHealth at report.md:327-337 has five members, none about nets. I7 (report.md:137-140) says "every failure net … use[s] exactly this surface", yet `attach()`/`detach()`/`BucketHealth` — the machinery that makes tap death visible — apply only to buckets. The plan register's "onerror handoff fights incumbents in both directions" recorded the clobbering direction as asked nowhere; still true of the report.

#### triggers-and-nets-5 — RecorderConfig.nets — default and the worked example

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Whether nets are armed by default is never stated, `nets` is optional, and the report's own worked configuration omits it — a recorder with no failure nets is not a config error and is invisible everywhere.
- **Mechanism**: `nets?: { windowErrors?: boolean; unhandledRejections?: boolean }` is optional with no documented default. The worked example (report.md:437-458) — presented as the built-ins' privilege audit and the canonical composition-root snippet an implementer will copy — sets `enabled`, `identity`, `redaction`, `trigger`, `buckets`, and `transport`, and never sets `nets`. Under fail-safe-loud (I8), an omitted optional key is not a rejection, so `configErrors` stays empty and the one-line startup assertion passes.
- **Effect**: The charter's headline capture path can be entirely absent in production with no configError, no health field, no bundle evidence, and a green startup assertion — and the report's own example is the artifact that propagates the omission.
- **Owner**: `partial:I8 — fail-safe-loud makes invalid rows loud but says nothing about an absent optional net declaration; Q6's health block covers buckets and config errors only`
- **Rationale**: I8 at report.md:141-146 is scoped to "an invalid bucket row" and table-level validation. The example config at report.md:437-458 contains no `nets` key while report.md:441 spells out trigger defaults "defaults, spelled out" — the omission is conspicuous against the example's own stated intent.

#### triggers-and-nets-6 — Q7 pre-arm stub — inline head script under a Content-Security-Policy

- **Mode** *(high)*: The pre-arm stub is prescribed as an inline `<script>` in the HTML head, and no fact about the app's CSP or how index.html is served has ever been elicited; under a policy without `'unsafe-inline'` and without a per-response nonce, the stub simply never runs.
- **Mechanism**: Q7 specifies "a pre-arm stub in the HTML head, before the app bundle: ~30 owned lines". A CSP `script-src` lacking `'unsafe-inline'` blocks inline execution; the violation is reported to the console and to any `report-to` endpoint, neither of which the recorder can see, and no error handler exists yet to observe it. Vite 8 emits a static index.html, so a nonce would have to be injected per response by whatever serves it — an unknown. The survey applied exactly this CSP lens to reject fast-redact as a dependency (report.md:25, 555) but not to its own prescribed inline script.
- **Effect**: The boot-window compensation is silently absent, and an empty replay queue at init is byte-identical to "no pre-boot errors occurred" — the report claims a coverage the deployment may not have, and the report's own boundedness/absence discipline (every gap must be visible) is broken at the one place it cannot be instrumented.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — no intake item covers CSP or index.html serving (items a–l cover payloads, redaction, endpoint, buckets, build id, hardware, incumbent handlers, Reporting-Endpoints, device id, tabs, workers, interruption), and the report's assumption list has no CSP assumption`
- **Rationale**: Grep for `csp|content-security|unsafe-inline|nonce|inline` across report.md returns only the fast-redact `unsafe-eval` findings (lines 25, 555) — the dependency lens — and nothing about the stub. intake/2026-08-22-0160-recorder-facts.md headers a–l contain no CSP item.

#### triggers-and-nets-7 — Q7 pre-arm stub — queue with no drain of its own

- **Mode** *(high)*: The stub queues pre-boot errors for the recorder to drain at init, so it yields evidence only for boot failures that boot survives — the failure class it exists for is precisely the class that prevents the drainer from existing.
- **Mechanism**: The stub "register[s] `error`/`unhandledrejection` listeners into a bounded array … which the recorder drains and replays into its own nets at init, then removes." It has no transport, no beacon, no storage. A chunk 404, a module-eval throw, or a config-wiring throw is observed by the stub (it is in the head, armed before the bundle) and then never delivered, because `createFlightRecorder` is never reached. Q7's own sentence misplaces the boundary — it files "bundle 404" under "boot-loop failures *before even the stub*", when the stub is already armed at that point.
- **Effect**: The report reads as if the boot window is covered; in the fatal-boot case the stub captures the evidence and takes it to the grave. Absence-based backend detection is the only remaining net, and it is explicitly backend work nobody in this track owns.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q7 — the queue-and-replay pattern is designed, but no last-gasp path exists for the case where the drainer never arrives, and Q7's boot-loop sentence asserts a boundary the stub's own position contradicts`
- **Rationale**: report.md:517: "~30 owned lines that register `error`/`unhandledrejection` listeners into a bounded array … which the recorder drains and replays … Boot-loop failures before even the stub (HTML did not parse, bundle 404) produce no report". The stub is in the head before the app bundle, so a bundle 404 occurs after the stub arms, not before it.

#### triggers-and-nets-8 — Q7 pre-arm replay → capture(); CaptureOptions / clockOrigin

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Replayed pre-boot errors have no representable original timestamp — `CaptureOptions` has no occurrence-time field and the bundle's clock origin is minted after the errors happened.
- **Mechanism**: `CaptureOptions` is `{ error?: unknown; context?: Record<string, unknown> }`. Every clock in the bundle derives from `header.clockOrigin` (RD-2/RD-3), stamped once at recorder construction; `sealedAt` is stamped at seal. A pre-boot error replayed at init therefore reports the init instant, and its monotonic offset relative to an origin minted later is unrepresentable (negative or fabricated). The only place an implementer can put the real time is `context`, which is untyped app data that also passes seal-time redaction.
- **Effect**: The ordering of the boot cascade — the exact thing a boot-failure bundle is read for — collapses to a single init timestamp, and the report's two-clock discipline (its strongest replay-fidelity commitment) has a hole exactly where the taps have not yet attached and the header is the only evidence.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — neither Q7's pre-arm design nor the interface artifact provides a trigger-time input; RD-2's two clocks are stamped by the recorder, never supplied`
- **Rationale**: CaptureOptions at report.md:312-317; `clockOrigin` at report.md:373-374 ("one shared clock domain; every record's monoMs offsets this origin"); Q7's replay sentence at report.md:517 says only "drains and replays into its own nets at init".

#### triggers-and-nets-9 — Q7 pre-arm replay × cooldown × attach order

- **Mode** *(high)*: Replaying N queued pre-boot errors at init spends the 30 s cooldown on the first one, over rings that are still empty — the rest of the boot cascade and the first 30 s of real incidents are discarded to a counter.
- **Mechanism**: Replay drives `capture()` once per queued error. The recorder is constructed first and taps attach "as [their] source constructs" (report.md:464-465, 89), so at replay time the mqtt/http/xstate/log rings hold nothing and several buckets have no `attachedSince`. The first replayed error seals a bundle whose buckets are empty-but-valid; every later replayed error returns `{outcome:'suppressed'}`, which carries no reason, no error, and no bundleId. A genuine startup failure at t+2 s (MQTT connect failure, first-render crash) is suppressed for the same reason.
- **Effect**: The boot window produces exactly one contentless bundle, and the errors that explain it — plus the first real incident of the session — survive only as `triggers.suppressed: N`. This is the worst-case interaction of two independently reasonable rulings (queue-and-replay, one bundle per cooldown).
- **Owner**: `partial:Q7 — cooldown-with-counted-suppression is Q7's ruling and replay is Q7's design, but nothing exempts replay, scales the first window, or lets the replay drain into one bundle instead of N triggers`
- **Rationale**: report.md:515 ("one bundle per cooldown window … suppressed triggers return a counted `{outcome: 'suppressed'}` receipt"), report.md:517 (replay at init), report.md:89 and 464-465 (recorder constructed first, taps attach later). No text couples the three.

#### triggers-and-nets-10 — Q7 cooldown; CaptureReceipt / RecorderHealth.triggers; I4

- **Mode** *(high)*: A suppressed trigger discards its identity entirely — reason, error, and time are all dropped, leaving only an integer — so I4's "a bundle accounts for every absence" holds inside buckets and fails at the trigger layer.
- **Mechanism**: `CaptureReceipt`'s suppressed arm is `{ outcome: 'suppressed' }` with no bundleId; `health().triggers` is `{sealed, coalesced, suppressed}`, three cumulative integers. Coalesced triggers do better — "the reason is appended to that bundle's header" (`coalescedReasons`) — but suppressed triggers append nothing anywhere. The design invented a per-name ledger for exactly this class of problem one layer down (`unknownBuckets: Record<string, number>`, "under loose TS the typo names itself") and did not apply it to suppressed trigger reasons.
- **Effect**: When the second incident inside 30 s is genuinely different (a distinct failure following the first, the informative one), the backend sees one incident and a number. The four-species drop ledger's whole claim — no uncounted, unnamed loss — is not honoured for triggers.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q7/I4 — the plan finding "cooldown-suppressed triggers are unaccounted" was answered with a count only; the design's own per-name ledger idiom exists and was not reused`
- **Rationale**: CaptureReceipt at report.md:319-323; `triggers` at report.md:332; `unknownBuckets` at report.md:334-336; I4 at report.md:126-128 ("A bundle accounts for every absence; there is no uncounted loss") is written over bucket records only.

#### triggers-and-nets-11 — Q7 cooldown keying × charter's classify-nothing rule

- **Mode** *(high)*: The cooldown is time-only and unkeyed, and the design adds no noise filter, so any benign global-net event — ResizeObserver loop, an extension-injected script error, an opaque `Script error.` — seals a full bundle and then suppresses the real incident for 30 s.
- **Mechanism**: `cooldownMs` is "suppression window after a seal", global, with no keying on reason, error message, or stack. The charter forbids classification ("classifies nothing and evaluates nothing"), so no ignore-list exists, and the report's Q7 acknowledges opaque cross-origin errors as a visibility ceiling without addressing their volume. Every such event is a first-class trigger: seal, assemble, gzip, POST a multi-MB bundle, then blind the recorder for the cooldown.
- **Effect**: Junk bundles occupy the one-in-flight-plus-one-pending delivery slots and the 8-attempt retry horizon while the incident that matters returns `{outcome:'suppressed'}` with no content. Noise does not merely waste budget — it preferentially destroys evidence.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q7 — cooldown owns storm-bounding; the plan's "classify-nothing recorder cannot filter net noise" mode is not addressed anywhere in the report, and no fact about third-party/cross-origin scripts was ever elicited`
- **Rationale**: report.md:185-192 (cooldown is a bare ms window), report.md:513 ("third-party scripts, if any, report opaquely" — treated as a yield caveat, not a volume hazard). Grep finds no `ResizeObserver`, no ignore-list, no filter anywhere in report.md; intake items a–l contain no cross-origin-scripts item.

#### triggers-and-nets-12 — Q7 nets — unhandledrejection semantics

- **Mode** *(medium)*: No `rejectionhandled` debounce: a promise handled one tick late fires `unhandledrejection` and seals a full bundle for a non-failure.
- **Mechanism**: `unhandledrejection` fires at the microtask checkpoint even when the app attaches a handler on a later tick; the browser then fires `rejectionhandled` to retract it. React data-fetching, retry wrappers, and orval-generated call sites produce this pattern routinely. The design's net triggers immediately and synchronously through the seal, with no pairing against `rejectionhandled` and no deferral of even one task.
- **Effect**: False-positive multi-MB bundles at fleet scale, each of which then consumes the cooldown per the mode above, so benign rejections systematically displace real incidents; `triggers.sealed` stops meaning "incidents".
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — `rejectionhandled` appears nowhere in the report; the plan-level mode was partial:Q7 and the design took no action on it`
