- **Rationale**: Grep of report.md for `rejectionhandled` returns nothing; `unhandledrejection` appears only as a net name (report.md:139, 198, 513, 616). Q7's protocol paragraph (report.md:515) is written entirely around cooldown and coalescing.

#### triggers-and-nets-13 — BundleHeader.trigger.error; CaptureOptions.error: unknown

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Non-Error trigger payloads have no projection path — the header's error block is a bare `{message, stack?}` and the bounded serializer built for exactly this problem is bucket-only.
- **Mechanism**: `CaptureOptions.error` is `unknown`; rejection reasons are routinely strings, plain objects, `Response`s, or the boundary's `BoundaryError` class. `BundleHeader.trigger.error` is `{ message: string; stack?: string; markers?: LossMarker[] }` — no field for `source`/`lineno`/`colno` (the only data `window.onerror` still has when `error` is null), and no route through the depth/breadth-bounded, cycle-safe, marker-stamping projection every bucket record gets. "The stack is parsed behind the seam" is the only rule.
- **Effect**: Either `String(reason)` yields "[object Object]" and the trigger's only evidence is destroyed, or an implementer JSON-stringifies the reason into `message` unbounded and unmetered — the well-formed-wrong-evidence failure RD-5 exists to prevent, in the field a reader looks at first.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9/I3 — the header's fields are enumerated and pass redaction at seal, but the meter is explicitly per-bucket and the projection contract of Q3 is written for bucket entries only`
- **Rationale**: CaptureOptions at report.md:312-317; header trigger block at report.md:376-379; I3 at report.md:122-125 meters "per bucket"; Q3's projection contract (report.md:72-78) is scoped to bucket scrubbers. Nothing routes `CaptureOptions.error` through the bounded serializer.

#### triggers-and-nets-14 — Q8 post-trigger window — BundleHeader.coalescedReasons

- **Mode** *(high)*: `coalescedReasons` is an unbounded, unmetered array fed at loop rate — the design's own coalescing rule turns an error loop into header growth in the one bundle component that has only a static allowance.
- **Mechanism**: During an open post-trigger window every trigger coalesces and "the reason is appended to that bundle's header". A React render loop or a rejecting-retry loop fires thousands of times per second; `coalescedReasons: readonly string[]` carries no cap and its entries are app-supplied strings governed by no budget. I3's envelope arithmetic is `Σ maxBytes + header allowance ≤ envelope`, validated statically at startup — nothing enforces the allowance at seal.
- **Effect**: During exactly the incident class cooldown was designed to bound, the header grows without limit on a RAM-disk device, blowing the peak-memory budget of Q9 and the endpoint's size ceiling. The plan's low-severity "app-supplied reason is unmetered" mode is amplified by a design element added after it.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q8/I3 — coalescing is Q8's ruling and the header allowance is an I3 validator constant; no cap, truncation rule, or drop counter for coalesced reasons exists in the interface`
- **Rationale**: report.md:515 ("the reason is appended to that bundle's header"), report.md:377 (`coalescedReasons: readonly string[]`), I3 at report.md:122-125 (static Σ validation, "never by cross-bucket eviction"). `DropCounters` has no trigger-layer species.

#### triggers-and-nets-15 — I9 / Q7 protocol — the synchronous seal lands inside a host's latency-disciplined turn

- **Mode** *(high)*: `capture()` is synchronous through the seal, so a trigger fired inside 0060's shared dispatch turn or an xstate processing turn executes the whole seal there — work Q11 budgets only for tap bodies.
- **Mechanism**: I9 makes capture synchronous through the seal; the protocol is a header stamp (including a tracekit stack parse and seal-time redaction of error/stack), an O(n) pointer copy per bucket — 18,000 records at the worked config's `maxCount`s — plus a ledger and correlation-open-set snapshot. Triggers reach that path from inside other people's turns: the worked example calls `recorder.capture('pump-controller-stuck', {error})` from a state machine, and a global error report for a throw inside a message handler runs the net synchronously in the same turn. 0060 sheds for every consumer past 256 queued events.
- **Effect**: The recorder's *trigger* path — not its capture path — can cause app-wide message shedding at the exact moment an incident is being recorded, which is the charter's forbidden outcome (degrading the app), reached through the one entry point Q11's latency discipline does not cover.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q11 — Q11's per-event budget is stated over "every tap body" and its seal hazard is budgeted for memory (Q9's streaming assembly) and frequency (cooldown), never for synchronous cost inside a host turn`
- **Rationale**: I9 at report.md:147-149; protocol at report.md:515; Q11's hazard list at report.md:543-546 — "every tap body is bounded … no delivery work on capture turns" and "the seal is a hazard and is budgeted as one: question 9's streaming assembly caps the spike; the cooldown caps seal frequency". Neither clause bounds seal latency. The spike list (report.md:624-628) measures projection cost and seal *correctness* under load, not seal latency in a host turn.

#### triggers-and-nets-16 — Seal — trigger-header assembly executes app code; DropCounters species

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Reading and redacting the trigger's own error can throw (getters, custom `Error` subclasses, `stack` accessors), and there is no counter or marker species for that failure.
- **Mechanism**: The seal stamps the header from `CaptureOptions.error`: reading `.message`/`.stack` invokes app or library getters, tracekit parses the stack string, and Q13's seal-time redaction walks the error text and `context`. I1 forbids `capture()` throwing, so any throw there must be swallowed — but `DropCounters` has four species, all per-bucket (`evicted`, `truncated`, `oversized`, `projectionFailures`), and `BundleHeader.trigger.error.markers` carries only `truncated`/`redacted`/`elided`. There is no "the trigger's own error could not be read" marker or counter.
- **Effect**: A bundle ships with an absent or partial error block and nothing anywhere says why — an uncounted loss at the single field the reader opens first, contradicting I4's central claim.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED — I1 forces the swallow, I4 forbids uncounted loss, and no counter or marker exists to reconcile them at the header layer`
- **Rationale**: I1 at report.md:117-119; DropCounters at report.md:352-357 (all four are bucket-scoped); LossMarker union at report.md:415-418; Q13's second application point at report.md:555 ("the seal applies the same rules to the envelope's own free text — the trigger's error message and stack").

#### triggers-and-nets-17 — Q11 self-capture cut 2 — the `sealing` re-entrancy flag

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The `sealing` flag's suppression is indistinguishable from cooldown suppression in both the receipt and health, and no invariant guarantees it is cleared — a flag stuck by one internal throw permanently disarms every net while looking like a busy cooldown.
- **Mechanism**: Q11 rules "a `sealing` boolean suppresses net-triggered re-seals, counted". Counted where? `health().triggers.suppressed` is the same integer cooldown increments, and `CaptureReceipt` has no re-entrant arm — only sealed/coalesced/suppressed/disabled. The flag is specified as a mechanism, not as an invariant with a clearing guarantee on every path (an internal throw mid-seal, an early return, a transport that throws synchronously before the flag is reset).
- **Effect**: Permanently dead nets that read as a healthy suppression count; no field in health or in any bundle can distinguish "we are suppressing re-entrant self-triggers" from "we are inside a cooldown" from "the flag never cleared and we have captured nothing for an hour".
- **Owner**: `partial:Q11 — the flag is named as the mechanism; its lifetime, clearing guarantee, and observable state are not in the interface`
- **Rationale**: report.md:545 (the three cuts); RecorderHealth.triggers at report.md:332; CaptureReceipt at report.md:319-323. No invariant I1–I10 covers flag lifecycle, and the spike list does not include re-entrancy.

#### triggers-and-nets-18 — Q11 self-capture cuts — asynchronous recorder failures

- **Mode** *(high)*: Q11 states its self-capture rule two contradictory ways, and the version that survives (the `sealing` flag) covers only the synchronous seal — every asynchronous recorder failure fires the nets indistinguishably from an app error.
- **Mechanism**: "The nets ignore errors whose stack tops in recorder frames during an active seal (re-entrancy flag, not stack parsing on the hot path…)" — the first clause describes stack inspection, the second refuses it. What remains is a boolean covering the synchronous seal only. Everything the recorder does off that turn is outside the flag: bundle assembly, the gzip worker (an unhandled error in a dedicated worker propagates to the parent window's `error` event), and a rejecting `transport` promise. Having refused stack parsing, the design has no mechanism left to recognise those as recorder-origin.
- **Effect**: A deterministic assembly, gzip, or delivery bug produces one self-caused bundle per cooldown window indefinitely, each failing the same way, each reported to the backend as an app incident — the recorder becoming the incident through the async half of its own pipeline.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q11 — three cuts are named (HTTP marker header, sealing flag, never-report-through-itself); the asynchronous cut does not exist and the design foreclosed the only identifying mechanism`
- **Rationale**: report.md:545 verbatim; I9 at report.md:147-149 puts "bundling, compression (worker-side), and delivery" behind the seam and off the record path, i.e. outside the flag's window; report.md:529 handles a dead gzip worker as a degradation path only ("degrades to the uncompressed body") without addressing the window `error` event it already fired.

#### triggers-and-nets-19 — Interface — RecorderConfig.transport; I1/I6

- **Mode** *(high)*: No invariant requires the recorder to catch the promise its injected `transport` returns, so a rejecting transport fires the recorder's own `unhandledRejections` net — a dead endpoint becomes a self-sustaining trigger loop.
- **Mechanism**: `transport: (bundle: SealedBundle) => void | Promise<void>` is app-supplied at the composition root, in a very-loose-TS codebase with heavy agent-authored churn, where `async (b) => { await fetch(...) }` with no try/catch is the default thing that gets written. Its rejection (endpoint 500, CORS preflight failure, abort at dismissal) becomes an unhandled rejection unless the recorder attaches a catch. I1's no-throw list is record/attach/detach/capture/health; I6 says the transport's "errors are its own to absorb" — a statement about the transport author's duty, not a recorder-side guarantee.
- **Effect**: Every failed delivery becomes a new trigger; the recorder reports its own delivery failure as an app incident at cooldown rate, forever, while the bounded-then-drop delivery loop keeps retrying underneath. This is precisely I6's "never reports through itself" defeated by a typing detail.
- **Owner**: `partial:I6 — the intent is stated in prose in the config doc comment; no invariant covers the returned promise, and the delivery-side spike (report.md:628) scripts a dead endpoint without checking whether it re-triggers the nets`
- **Rationale**: transport signature and doc comment at report.md:200-205; I1 at report.md:117-119; I6 at report.md:132-136. Q10 (report.md:533) describes retry/backoff behind the seam but never states that the recorder awaits-and-catches the caller's function.

#### triggers-and-nets-20 — react-net.ts — `createRecorderBoundary` semantics

- **Mode** *(high)*: The report says the boundary "calls `capture()` and rethrows-to-render-fallback", which is self-contradictory; the rethrow reading converts every boundary-caught error into a full-tree unmount.
- **Mechanism**: report.md:513 says rethrow; the exported signature is `ComponentType<{ fallback: ReactNode; children?: ReactNode }>` — a boundary that renders `fallback` does not rethrow, and a boundary that rethrows from `componentDidCatch` propagates the error to the next boundary above or, at the root, unmounts the entire React tree in React 18. An implementer working from the prose in a loose-TS, agent-authored codebase can plausibly implement either.
- **Effect**: Under the rethrow reading, mounting the recorder's own boundary — the component the integration checklist tells the app to mount — turns recoverable component errors into whole-app blank screens. That is the charter's one forbidden outcome, delivered by the recorder's only React-facing export.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q7 — the prose and the interface artifact disagree, and no invariant rules boundary rethrow/recovery semantics (I10 only says components reach the recorder through this adapter)`
- **Rationale**: report.md:513 ("ships `<RecorderErrorBoundary>` that calls `capture()` and rethrows-to-render-fallback") against report.md:424-430 (the adapter's signature takes and presumably renders `fallback`). I10 at report.md:150-152 addresses layering only.

#### triggers-and-nets-21 — react-net.ts — boundary placement and coverage evidence

- **Mode** *(high)*: An app boundary nearer the throw shadows the recorder's boundary, and a recorder boundary inside a lazy subtree that never mounts is inert — neither state is representable anywhere in health, config errors, or the bundle.
- **Mechanism**: React invokes only the nearest boundary above the throwing component. The app is React 18.3.1 with heavy agent-authored churn, and intake item g asks only about boundaries "with reporting side effects", so an ordinary `<ErrorBoundary fallback={…}>` is both unelicited and undetectable. The report's answer — "the report's integration checklist counts mounted boundaries as config" — is prose: `RecorderConfig` has no boundary declaration, `configErrors` cannot flag a missing mount, and `RecorderHealth`/`BundleHeader` carry no boundary block. Every other absence in this design is machinery-visible (`attachedSince`, `attachEpoch`, `configErrors`, `unknownBuckets`); this one is not.
- **Effect**: Component-tree errors are swallowed by an app boundary while the bundle reads as "no component errors ever happen", and a boundary in a route chunk that never loads has the identical signature. The charter's third net can be 0%-effective with no observable difference from 100%.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q7 + intake g — g elicits only incumbents with reporting side effects, and the design's answer to boundary coverage is a checklist rather than the health machinery it uses for every other liveness question`
- **Rationale**: report.md:513 ("the report's integration checklist counts mounted boundaries as config"); RecorderConfig at report.md:158-206 has no boundary field; RecorderHealth at report.md:327-337 has none either; intake/2026-08-22-0160-recorder-facts.md item g asks about "React error boundaries with reporting side effects".

#### triggers-and-nets-22 — react-net.ts × Q7 cooldown — one incident, two capture() calls

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Nested recorder boundaries and React 18's synchronous retry of a failed concurrent render produce two `capture()` calls for one incident, and the design's time-only cooldown discards the second — which is often the more informative one.
- **Mechanism**: React 18 retries a concurrent render that threw synchronously, so the same error can reach a boundary twice; separately, when a fallback itself throws, the error propagates to the next recorder boundary above, producing a second, *different* error. In both cases the first call seals and the second returns `{outcome:'suppressed'}` (or coalesces, if a post-trigger window is open), retaining nothing. If the two land either side of a cooldown expiry instead, two near-duplicate multi-MB bundles ship.
- **Effect**: The duplicate case wastes a bundle; the fallback-error case destroys the record of why the recovery UI also failed — the recorder is blindest exactly where the app degraded twice.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q7 — cooldown deduplicates by time with no key, which is correct for true duplicates and wrong for a distinct second error arriving inside the window; no dedup key or reason ledger exists`
- **Rationale**: report.md:515 (cooldown is a time window; suppressed carries no reason) with the suppressed-identity mode above; nothing in Q7 or react-net addresses nested boundaries, fallback failure, or React's retry behaviour.

#### triggers-and-nets-23 — Q7 net during dismissal × Q9 last-gasp marker ordering

- **Mode** *(medium)*: A net firing during page dismissal produces neither a bundle nor a loss marker, because the marker is emitted on an event that has already fired.
- **Mechanism**: Q9's last-gasp marker rides `visibilitychange`-hidden (paired with `pagehide`) and is sent "only when a sealed bundle is in flight or pending". An error thrown in teardown/unload code fires the net *after* that handler ran, when nothing was in flight, so no marker was sent; the resulting seal is synchronous and succeeds, but assembly, optional gzip, and the POST are async (I9) and the page dies first. Firefox at the ~124 floor has no `fetch keepalive`, so the async POST cannot survive dismissal either.
- **Effect**: The dismissal-window failure class — teardown errors, unload-time rejections, aborted in-flight work — produces nothing at all, and the design's own loss-reporting compensation is ordered before the trigger it should compensate, so the loss is silent rather than reported.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — the marker's emission condition and timing are ruled, but a trigger *originating* during dismissal falls between Q7's async pipeline and Q9's in-flight rule, exactly as the plan-level mode said`
- **Rationale**: report.md:527 ("sent on `visibilitychange`-hidden (paired with `pagehide` per current guidance) only when a sealed bundle is in flight or pending"); I9 at report.md:147-149 (delivery is behind the seam, never synchronous); report.md:527 also fixes `sendBeacon`-only at the floor.

#### triggers-and-nets-24 — Q7 pre-arm stub — the queue itself; I2 and I4

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The stub holds raw error references with no projection and no ledger — it violates the design's two central invariants in the one component that runs before either can apply, and its overflow is the only truly uncounted loss in the design.
- **Mechanism**: The stub is "~30 owned lines" registering listeners "into a bounded array (count-capped)". Those entries are live `ErrorEvent`/rejection-reason references: serialize-at-capture (I2) does not apply, so the array pins each error's closure scope and whatever graph it reaches for the whole boot window; count-cap overflow drops silently, and there is no path for a pre-arm drop counter to reach `health()` or `BundleHeader` — no `preArm` field exists in either.
- **Effect**: Boot-window memory retention nobody meters, and the first pre-boot errors (the earliest and usually most causal) evicted with no counter — in a design whose central claim is that every absence is accounted for.
- **Owner**: `partial:I2/I4 — both invariants are scoped to `record()` and the ring; the stub is outside the seam by construction, and the interface offers no field to carry its ledger into a bundle`
- **Rationale**: report.md:517 (stub description); I2 at report.md:120-121 ("When record() returns, the recorder holds no reference to the caller's entry"); I4 at report.md:126-128; RecorderHealth at report.md:327-337 and BundleHeader at report.md:366-386 have no pre-arm block.

#### triggers-and-nets-25 — Interface — dispose() × armed nets × mounted boundaries

- **Mode** *(low)*: `dispose()` disarming chained nets clobbers any net armed after it, and boundaries created by `createRecorderBoundary` keep calling a disposed recorder with no receipt that says so.
- **Mechanism**: `dispose()` "disarms the nets it armed". Under property-assignment chaining, disarming means restoring the handler saved at arm time — which blindly overwrites a net armed later by a second recorder (HMR re-eval, a double composition root, a test harness). Meanwhile `createRecorderBoundary(recorder)` closes over the handle: mounted boundaries survive disposal and call `capture()` on a dead recorder. `CaptureReceipt` has no `disposed` arm, and `health().enabled` is the kill-switch bit, not disposal state.
- **Effect**: In dev/HMR — the environment where error boundaries are actually exercised and where agent-authored churn is heaviest — the app can end up with no armed nets and boundaries reporting into a dead handle, with every observable surface saying the recorder is enabled and healthy.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q6 — dispose is declared "composition-root lifecycle only (tests, HMR)" with no stated post-dispose semantics for capture(), no re-arm safety rule, and no health representation`
- **Rationale**: dispose at report.md:260-262; `createRecorderBoundary` at report.md:428-430; CaptureReceipt at report.md:319-323; RecorderHealth.enabled at report.md:328 documented against `enabled` in RecorderConfig (report.md:161-163) as the kill switch.

### Delivery and the transport seam (25 modes)

#### delivery-and-transport-1 — Q10 delivery loop / transport slot — the POST itself

- **Mode** *(high)*: The report POST is specified as a "bare fetch" with no request timeout, so a hung endpoint pins the in-flight delivery slot for the life of the tab.
- **Mechanism**: Q10 line 537: "the report POST is a bare `fetch` outside 0060's boundary". `fetch()` has no default timeout in any browser. A flapping endpoint that completes the TCP/TLS handshake and then stalls (half-open connection, LB with no idle reaper, a proxy holding the request) leaves the promise unsettled indefinitely. Q10's termination rule — "bounded retries, then drop with a counter: default 8 attempts / ~5 minutes horizon" — counts *attempts*, and attempt 1 never completes, so the horizon never elapses. Nothing in the report or the interface mentions `AbortSignal.timeout` (grep: zero hits for "timeout", "AbortSignal" outside the HTTP-bucket abort discussion), even though `AbortSignal.timeout` is available at the Firefox ~124 floor (FF 100+).
- **Effect**: The whole delivery machine wedges on the first hung POST: the in-flight slot is never released, every subsequent seal lands in the pending slot and is evicted by the next one, `deliveryFailed` never increments (nothing failed), and the sealed bundle's record strings — which the rings have since evicted, so the bundle is now their sole owner — stay resident for the session. This is precisely the pinned-memory outcome Q10 chose bounded-then-drop to avoid, on a RAM-disk device, and it arrives silently.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q10 owns retry termination and rules it by attempt count and a wall-clock "horizon", but the horizon is asserted, not mechanised: no text anywhere in the report bounds a single attempt's duration. Q9's budget-one paragraph describes the carrier as "an ordinary async `fetch` POST" and discusses only size and compression. The spike list's delivery lane tests "bounded-then-drop against a scripted dead endpoint" — a *dead* endpoint rejects fast; a *hung* endpoint is the untested case, and the spike as scoped will not surface it.

#### delivery-and-transport-2 — Q10 delivery loop — response handling

- **Mode** *(high)*: The retry loop has no permanent-versus-transient classification, so a structurally impossible delivery (413, 400 schema mismatch, 401, a CORS preflight the endpoint will never satisfy) is retried 8 times at envelope scale before dropping.
- **Mechanism**: Q10 specifies backoff, jitter, one-at-a-time, and "default 8 attempts / ~5 minutes horizon, then the bundle is freed". No text distinguishes response classes — grep finds no "413", no "status code", no permanent/transient language anywhere in the report. Three concrete generators: (a) gzip disabled by default (Q9) means an uncompressed body 5–10× the planning figure, which can exceed the endpoint's ceiling (intake c, unanswered) and 413 forever; (b) a build-rollout skew where the backend validates a newer vendored OpenAPI version 400s a bundle valid per the build that produced it; (c) a cross-origin preflight failure surfaces in `fetch` as an opaque `TypeError`, identical to a network error, so it *cannot* be distinguished from a transient fault without inspecting the response the browser refuses to give.
- **Effect**: Up to 8 × (up to 50 MB) uploaded from a fleet device for a delivery that can never succeed — bandwidth and CPU spent during a degraded moment — while the pending slot churns and discards newer evidence for the full 5-minute horizon. A truncated or re-chunked bundle would have been accepted; the design has no downsize-and-resend path, and the resulting `deliveryFailed` count cannot tell the team the loss was recoverable.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q10 — owns the retry count and drop, but no response-class handling; partial:Q9 — owns the size ceiling as a *sizing* input (intake c) and never the client's reaction to rejection`
- **Rationale**: The plan-level pass raised this as "Oversized bundle rejected; retry loop cannot ever succeed" (partial:Q9). The design's answer is only sizing-side: "ship uncompressed by default; enable gzip only when intake item c confirms the endpoint decodes it". That reduces one generator (b/c remain) and does nothing about the client's behaviour on rejection. The plan-level disposition is therefore not answered by the design.

#### delivery-and-transport-3 — Q10 delivery loop — server backpressure

- **Mode** *(high)*: Server-signalled rate limiting (429 / `Retry-After`, or a 503 with `Retry-After`) is not honoured; the client backs off only on its own schedule, and a fleet-wide incident becomes a fleet-wide upload storm against the report endpoint.
- **Mechanism**: Grep: "429" and "Retry-After" appear nowhere in the report. Q10's backoff is the copied loglevel-plugin-remote idiom — a purely client-side exponential schedule. The survey read Sentry's transport source in detail and copied its *client-report loss counters* (Survey, Sentry section; Q2) but not the rate-limit half of the same transport (`X-Sentry-Rate-Limits`/`Retry-After` parsing), which is the part that protects the receiver. Combine with Q16's refusal of cross-tab coordination and the fleet shape: one device-level cause (a bad deploy, a broker outage the app reports on) fires nets in every tab on every device at once; each seals up to a 50 MB bundle and starts an independent 8-attempt schedule.
- **Effect**: The recorder's delivery amplifies the incident it is reporting: tens of devices × tens of MB in a phase-aligned burst against an endpoint that is already the thing failing, with the only remedy a startup-config change plus a reload of every device (Q11's kill switch is construction-time by ruling). "A recorder must never become the incident" fails at the delivery stage.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q10 owns delivery policy and enumerates backoff, jitter, slot bounds, termination, at-least-once, and independence — none of it receiver-directed. Q11's hazard list is closed and covers capture cost, per-tap throw containment, self-capture, the seal spike, and the kill switch; endpoint load is not in it. Q16 explicitly declines client-side coordination and hands dedup to the backend "for reporting purposes", which addresses storage, not load.

#### delivery-and-transport-4 — Q6 interface artifact vs Q10 prose — the delivery ledger

- **Mode** *(high)*: The `deliveryFailed` counter Q10 promises has no field anywhere in the interface, so the design's own artifact cannot carry the number its delivery section says rides in the next bundle.
- **Mechanism**: Q10: "the drop increments `deliveryFailed`, and the counter rides the next bundle and the last-gasp loss marker." The interface's `DropCounters` has exactly four members — `evicted`, `truncated`, `oversized`, `projectionFailures`. `RecorderHealth` has `enabled`, `configErrors`, `buckets`, `triggers`, `unknownBuckets`. `BundleHeader` carries `health: RecorderHealth` plus identity, clocks, trigger, `configDigest`, `configErrors`. There is no delivery block, no `deliveryFailed`, and no evicted-pending counter, at any level. Grep confirms `deliveryFailed` appears once in the entire report, in Q10 prose.
- **Effect**: Either the ledger invariant I4 ("A bundle accounts for every absence; there is no uncounted loss") is false for the delivery stage, or the OpenAPI contract seeded from `SealedBundle` ships without the field and adding it later is a cross-client/backend schema revision — the exact cheap-now/unrecoverable-later class RD-7 exists to catch. In the meantime delivery loss is invisible in every surface the design exposes: the receipt says `sealed`, `health()` says nothing, the bundle header says nothing.
- **Owner**: `partial:I4 — states the reconciliation invariant but scopes it to capture-stage drops; partial:Q10 — names the counter without a carrier`
- **Rationale**: The four-species ledger is defined in Q2 and I4 explicitly as capture-stage species ("evictions (ring pressure), truncations, oversized, projection failures"). Q10 introduces a fifth species in prose. The interface — the artifact the report says is the OpenAPI contract's seed ("The sealed bundle (input to the transport seam; the OpenAPI contract's seed)") — was not amended to match. This is an internal contradiction, checkable by reading the two texts side by side.

#### delivery-and-transport-5 — Q6 transport slot / Q10 — where the delivery policy actually lives

- **Mode** *(high)*: Every delivery rule the report recommends — one-in-flight-plus-one-pending, evict-older-pending, 8 attempts, at-least-once, the loss-marker beacon, worker gzip — lives inside an app-supplied closure typed `(bundle: SealedBundle) => void | Promise<void>`, which the recorder neither specifies, validates, nor observes.
- **Mechanism**: `RecorderConfig.transport` is a single function property; its doc comment says "Retry/backoff/one-at-a-time, worker-side gzip, and the wire format live behind it", and Q10 confirms "All of this lives behind question 6's `transport` seam — it is delivery policy, invisible to the capture surface." The recorder calls it once per sealed bundle and gets back void-or-promise. It cannot know how many attempts happened, whether a pending slot exists, whether the app's implementation kept a reference to the `SealedBundle` (the `readonly` markers are compile-time only, and the app's TypeScript is "very loose" with "a high volume of agent-authored code" per facts/app-profile.md), or whether the beacon listener was ever registered. Nothing in the interface says who frees the bundle after delivery, so both parties can believe the other owns the slot.
- **Effect**: The report's delivery recommendation is unenforceable and untestable through the seam it recommends: a transport that queues every bundle "for debugging", retries forever, or never retries is fully conformant to the type. Under agent churn the slot bounds and the retry horizon are the first things to drift, and the drift is invisible in `health()`, in the bundle, and in the receipt. The spike's delivery lane can only test a reference transport the app is under no obligation to match.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q10 — states the policy as the recommendation; partial:I6 — states delivery obligations as prose requirements on injected code ("Its errors are its own to absorb") with no mechanism`
- **Rationale**: The interface deliberately makes delivery opaque ("the interface cannot tell" whether it is owned fetch+backoff or a wrapped SDK transport) — a defensible posture-independence choice for the *survey*, but it means Q10's ruled choices are recommendations to an integrator rather than properties of the design. No question owns the conformance surface: Q15's falsification list tests "the delivery loop's bounded-then-drop under a scripted dead endpoint", i.e. an implementation, not the seam's contract.

#### delivery-and-transport-6 — Q6 config validation (I8) — the transport slot

- **Mode** *(high)*: A missing, non-function, or throwing `transport` is not in the config validator's stated scope, so the recorder comes up "healthy", returns `{outcome: 'sealed'}` receipts forever, and never delivers anything.
- **Mechanism**: I8 and Q2 define the validator's behaviour for bucket rows ("An invalid bucket row disables that bucket") and for one table-level case ("a config whose Σ maxBytes exceeds the envelope ... the recorder comes up disabled-loud"). Neither text mentions `transport`. Under the app's loose TypeScript an `any`-shaped startup config with a typo'd or absent `transport` compiles. At the first seal the recorder calls a non-function; I1 forbids a throw crossing the seam outward, so the failure is swallowed. `configErrors` stays empty, so the prescribed one-line startup guard `console.assert(recorder.health().configErrors.length === 0, …)` passes.
- **Effect**: Total, permanently silent delivery loss with every observable surface reporting success — the receipt says `sealed`, health says clean, the startup assertion passes. This is the worst variant of the report's own risk #6 ("A misconfigured bucket table is the likeliest real-world failure given the app's loose TS") applied to the one config field whose failure loses 100% of the evidence rather than one bucket's.
- **Owner**: `partial:I8 — the fail-safe-loud posture is ruled, but its enumerated scope is bucket rows plus the envelope sum; the transport slot is never brought inside it`
- **Rationale**: I8's text: "An invalid bucket row disables that bucket; every rejection appears in health().configErrors and in every bundle header." Q2's table-level case is specifically the Σ maxBytes violation. The design panel's recorded refusals (Q6) discuss fail-fast versus fail-safe for *construction*, and the worked example passes `transport: postBundleToReportEndpoint` without comment. Nothing states what a validator should check about it, and `CaptureReceipt` has no variant that could report it.

#### delivery-and-transport-7 — Q11 loop protection × Q10 transport rejection

- **Mode** *(high)*: A rejected transport promise surfaces as an unhandled rejection, which the recorder's own `unhandledRejections` net converts into a new trigger — a delivery-driven bundle mill the re-entrancy flag cannot see.
- **Mechanism**: `transport` returns `void | Promise<void>`; I6 says "Its errors are its own to absorb", but nothing enforces it, and an app transport that lets its final `fetch` rejection escape (or that throws inside a `.then`) produces an `unhandledrejection` event. `RecorderConfig.nets.unhandledRejections` arms exactly that event as a trigger. Q11's loop protection is "a `sealing` boolean suppresses net-triggered re-seals" plus "the nets ignore errors whose stack tops in recorder frames during an active seal". A delivery rejection fires minutes after the seal completed — `sealing` is false — and its stack tops in app transport code, not recorder frames. So: seal → assemble → POST fails → rejection → net → `capture()` → seal → …, gated only by the 30 s cooldown.
- **Effect**: With the endpoint down, the recorder generates a self-sustaining trigger every cooldown window, each costing a full seal, an envelope-scale assembly, a gzip pass, and a POST attempt burst — permanent CPU and memory load caused entirely by the recorder, on a degraded RAM-disk device, with every resulting bundle undeliverable. The bundles also fill with the previous cycle's reason, so the evidence is self-referential noise.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q11 — enumerates "the global nets must not re-trigger on the recorder's own throw" and mechanises it with a seal-scoped re-entrancy flag that is by construction false during async delivery`
- **Rationale**: Q11's three cuts are the header-marker self-capture filter, the `sealing` flag, and "the recorder never reports through itself". All three are scoped to the synchronous seal turn or to the HTTP tap. The delivery stage's asynchrony is exactly what defeats the flag, and I6's "its errors are its own to absorb" is a requirement placed on code the recorder does not own — the plan-level pass's "Recorder's own throw re-enters through the global net" was dispositioned Q11-owned for the *capture* path, and the design's mechanism does not extend to the delivery path.

#### delivery-and-transport-8 — I3 envelope validation × Q10 slot bounds

- **Mode** *(high)*: The in-flight and pending bundle slots hold up to two envelope-sized copies of evidence that the statically validated envelope does not count, so steady-state footprint reaches ~3× the configured envelope.
- **Mechanism**: I3: "The global 10–50 MB envelope is enforced STATICALLY by the config validator (Σ maxBytes + header allowance ≤ envelope), never by cross-bucket eviction." The allowance named is the *header*. Q10 then adds two bundle slots. Q9's memory paragraph is explicitly about the *transient* at seal ("sealed record copies (pointer copies; the strings are shared), the assembled body string, and its worker copy") and concludes the peak is "roughly envelope + largest-bucket". The sharing claim is true only at the seal instant: at ~50 msg/s the rings roll over within seconds-to-minutes, after which the sealed bundle is the sole owner of its strings. During a 5-minute retry horizon with a 30 s cooldown, an in-flight bundle plus a pending bundle plus the live rings are three independent envelope-scale allocations.
- **Effect**: "Bounded by construction" — the property the RAM-disk premise makes a safety property rather than a style preference — is false for the component this track adds, in exactly the sustained-outage scenario Q10 designed for. On a device whose OS lives in RAM, a 50 MB envelope becomes a ~150 MB resident recorder, and a full RAM disk is an OS-level failure.
- **Owner**: `partial:I3 — validates only Σ bucket bytes + a header allowance; partial:Q9 — budgets the seal-time transient and not the retry-window residency`
- **Rationale**: The plan-level pass raised "Pending bundle's bytes live outside the envelope's denominator" (partial:Q10). The design's response is Q9's streaming-assembly paragraph, which addresses peak-at-seal only, and Q10's bounded-then-drop, which caps residency at ~5 minutes per bundle rather than removing it — and simultaneously *adds* a second slot the plan did not have. The plan-level disposition is therefore made worse, not answered.

#### delivery-and-transport-9 — Q9 loss marker × Q10 bounded-then-drop

- **Mode** *(high)*: The loss marker fires only "when a sealed bundle is in flight or pending", so bundles already dropped by retry exhaustion generate no marker at all — the compensation for refusing durable parking does not cover the loss it was chosen to compensate.
- **Mechanism**: Q9: the beacon is "sent on `visibilitychange`-hidden (paired with `pagehide` per current guidance) only when a sealed bundle is in flight or pending." Q10's termination frees the bundle after ~5 minutes and "the drop increments `deliveryFailed`, and the counter rides the next bundle and the last-gasp loss marker." Sequence: endpoint down; bundle A exhausts and is freed; pending Z is promoted and exhausts; no further trigger fires (the incident resolved, or the user walks away). At dismissal there is nothing in flight and nothing pending, so no marker is sent. There is also no later bundle to carry `deliveryFailed`.
- **Effect**: An entire session's worth of incidents is lost with zero signal — no bundle, no marker, no counter, nothing at the backend but silence, which absence-based detection (Q7) will read as an ordinary session end rather than a loss. Q10's stated compensation for refusing durable parking — "the loss marker (question 9) is the compensation: losses are reported, not silent" — is false for precisely the exhaustion path Q10 itself introduced.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — defines the marker and its firing condition; partial:Q10 — asserts the marker as the compensation without checking the condition holds after exhaustion`
- **Rationale**: The plan-level pass raised "Dropped-bundle counter has no carrier if no later bundle ships" (partial:Q6, low). The design promotes the loss marker to the answer, and the answer's own gating condition reintroduces the gap in a specific, common case. The two texts contradict each other on the page.

#### delivery-and-transport-10 — Q9 loss marker — firing trigger

- **Mode** *(high)*: `visibilitychange`→hidden fires on ordinary tab switches, minimises, and app switches, not only at dismissal, so the marker reports a loss for bundles that are still delivering and will succeed.
- **Mechanism**: The Page Visibility event fires on every backgrounding. On a fleet device the user switching to another tab or app while a multi-MB POST is uploading — or while the bundle sits in a two-minute backoff wait, which is where most of an 8-attempt/5-minute schedule is spent — satisfies "a sealed bundle is in flight or pending" exactly. The design pairs `pagehide` but states no rule for distinguishing hidden-from-switch from hidden-from-dismissal (in-page they are not distinguishable; `pagehide` with `persisted === false` is the closest available signal), and states no once-per-bundleId rule, so repeated switches send repeated markers for the same `bundleId`.
- **Effect**: The backend receives `sealedButUndelivered: <bundleId>` markers for bundles that arrive normally moments later — false loss reports, at whatever rate the operator switches tabs. Because the same identity fields key Q7's absence-based crash detection, the false positives land in the one signal the report says compensates for Firefox producing no crash reports at the floor. And the marker's honest content is weaker than Q9 claims: at hidden-time the page cannot know the bundle will fail, so the beacon converts "bundle never arrived" not into "a positively-reported loss" but into "a bundle was outstanding" — a prediction the backend must reconcile against the bundle by id, a reconciliation requirement the report never states to the endpoint's owners.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — chooses the event and the gating condition, and asserts the semantics ("converts ... into a positively-reported loss") that the event's real firing behaviour does not support`
- **Rationale**: Q9's sentence is the whole specification. Nothing in Q9, Q10, or the spike list mentions tab-switch firing, marker dedup, or backend reconciliation; the spike list's real-browser lane defers "visibilitychange-hidden flush timing" to 0120's future harness, so the check that would catch this is named as unreachable. The at-least-once dedup rule stated to the endpoint owners covers bundles, not markers.

#### delivery-and-transport-11 — Q9 budget two — the 64 KiB shared quota

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The loss-marker beacon competes for the 64 KiB shared keepalive quota with 0050's facade remote sink, which flushes at the same dismissal moment for the same reason.
- **Mechanism**: Q9 states the fact — "the 64 KiB quota is shared across all in-flight keepalive-class requests, body lengths count, over-quota rejects as a network error" — and then designs the marker against it in isolation. The quota is per fetch group, i.e. per document: 0050's facade owns remote sinks (D-0014) and will flush error-level logs at dismissal; on floor Firefox both systems' only dismissal-surviving sender is `sendBeacon`. The moments are correlated by construction: the same incident produces both the recorder trigger and the error-level log burst. Whichever fires second gets `sendBeacon() === false`, and the design specifies no handling for that return — at dismissal there is nothing left to try.
- **Effect**: The last-gasp marker is silently lost exactly in the two-system failure scenarios where it matters most, and its loss is unobservable from either side. Q14's boundary statement — what the recorder shares with the facade is "the redaction rule set, the copied backoff design, and the log bucket's sink seam — and nothing else" — is wrong: they also share a hard 64 KiB device-level budget at the same instant.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: The plan-level pass raised this as partial:Q9 and the register's "What was not acted on" section named it explicitly as an item "the surveyor should read before answering Q2, Q7, Q9, Q10 and Q14" ("the sendBeacon quota shared with 0050's remote sink at dismissal"). Grep of the report: "quota" appears twice, both inside Q9's own budget-two sentence; "remote sink" appears once, in Q1, unrelated. The design did not act on it, and by adding a beacon it made the contention concrete rather than hypothetical.

#### delivery-and-transport-12 — Q9 budget two — the marker's wire format

- **Mode** *(high)*: The loss marker is a second, undocumented wire format sent to the report endpoint with no schema, no format version, no place in the vendored OpenAPI contract, and no intake question asking whether the endpoint accepts it.
- **Mechanism**: Q9 specifies the marker as `{identity, trigger reason, per-bucket drop counters, sealedButUndelivered: bundleId}` in "JSON bytes in a `text/plain` Blob". The vendored OpenAPI contract is seeded from `SealedBundle` (interface comment: "the OpenAPI contract's seed"); the marker is not a `SealedBundle` and appears nowhere in the interface. Q10's D-0006 argument covers "the bundle" being "validated against the generated schema in tests and in the spike" — the marker is not. Intake item c asks four things about the endpoint (origin, max size + gzip, auth, infra sharing) and nothing about a second payload shape or a second route. `BundleHeader.formatVersion` exists for RD-7; the marker has no analogue.
- **Effect**: An endpoint built to the contract will reject or silently discard `text/plain` bodies it has no route or parser for, so the entire compensation for refusing durable parking never lands — and because a beacon's response is unobservable (mode `no-cors`, opaque), the client can never learn this. Evolving the marker later has no version handle. This is the same silent-second-format hazard the report itself flags against Bugsnag's payload-version skew.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q9 decides what rides budget two; Q10 decides delivery and the D-0006 posture; neither brings the marker inside the contract, the schema-validation argument, or the intake. "What this track does not decide" scopes the backend out beyond "accepts the contracted payload over HTTP" — and the marker is by construction not the contracted payload, so no question can own it there either.

#### delivery-and-transport-13 — Q10 at-least-once × Q9 dedup key

- **Mode** *(high)*: The correctness of at-least-once delivery rests on two distinct backend behaviours nobody has asked for, and intake item c requests neither.
- **Mechanism**: Q9: "the backend upserts on `(deviceId, pageIncarnationId, bundleId)`". Q16: "the backend dedups by `(deviceId, trigger time-bucket, reason)` for reporting purposes, which costs nothing client-side". Q10: "Delivery is **at-least-once**; the report says so to the endpoint's owners (intake item c)". Intake item c (read in full) asks: same-origin or cross-origin, max payload size and gzip acceptance, auth, infrastructure sharing. It does not ask whether the endpoint can upsert, whether it has a natural key, whether it is append-only storage, or who owns changing it. `deviceId` itself is assumed to be the MQTT clientId (intake item i, also unanswered).
- **Effect**: If the endpoint is append-only — the common case for a log-shaped intake — every ambiguous outcome (2xx lost, connection dropped after persist) stores a duplicate incident, and every multi-tab device stores N near-duplicates. Incident counts inflate, any alerting built on them double-fires, and the client-side design that was chosen *because* the backend would dedup (bounded retries, no parking, N-tab duplication accepted) rests on an unverified premise. Discovering it after the contract freezes makes the fix a backend schema and storage change, not a client change.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q10 — declares the semantics and says to tell the endpoint owners; partial:Q9 — names the key; UNOWNED for the fact itself`
- **Rationale**: The plan-level pass's single UNOWNED delivery mode was "Duplicate delivery: same bundle stored twice by the backend". The design's answer is to add identity and delegate dedup to the backend — genuinely better — but it does not convert the delegation into a fact request, and the report's own "Assumptions in place of facts" list contains nothing about backend dedup capability while listing nine other assumptions. The dependency is load-bearing and undeclared.

#### delivery-and-transport-14 — Q16 × Q10 — what backend dedup discards

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The dedup key Q16 prescribes to the backend collapses exactly the distinct-evidence duplicates Q16 refused client-side coordination in order to preserve.
- **Mechanism**: Q16 refuses BroadcastChannel cooldown sharing because it is "wrong-by-default (suppressing tab B's bundle because tab A reported loses tab B's distinct history — the near-duplicates carry different evidence)", then in the same paragraph prescribes "the backend dedups by `(deviceId, trigger time-bucket, reason)` for reporting purposes". Two tabs failing on the same device from the same cause within one time bucket produce the same `reason` string and collide on that key, while carrying different xstate/HTTP/log history — the very evidence the refusal was protecting. Whether "dedups" means group-and-keep-both or discard-one is not stated, and the two readings have opposite consequences.
- **Effect**: If the backend discards on the key, the design loses client-side what it refused to lose client-side, one hop later and less visibly. If it groups, the word "dedups" is misleading to the endpoint owners who will implement it from this sentence. Either way the argument Q16 uses to justify refusing coordination does not survive its own prescription.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q16 — states both the refusal's rationale and the backend rule without reconciling them`
- **Rationale**: Both quotations are from the same paragraph of question 16. No other text disambiguates 'dedup' for the cross-tab key, and Q9's upsert key `(deviceId, pageIncarnationId, bundleId)` — which *would* keep both tabs, since `pageIncarnationId` differs — is a different key in a different section, so an implementer reading only Q16 gets the lossy one.

#### delivery-and-transport-15 — Q9 assembly / compression — worker lifecycle

- **Mode** *(high)*: Worker creation failing or hanging at seal time degrades to a main-thread path that rebuilds the whole envelope-sized body — re-admitting the 2–3× memory peak the worker existed to prevent, at the moment the device is likeliest already degraded.
- **Mechanism**: Q9 gives the worker two jobs: it is the compressor *and* the assembler — "assembly streams per bucket into the worker (bucket-sized chunks, transferable buffers) rather than building one envelope-sized string on the main thread, capping the peak at roughly envelope + largest-bucket rather than 2–3× envelope". Two sentences later: "a dead gzip worker degrades to the uncompressed body on the main-thread path, never to silent loss." The main-thread path is by definition the envelope-sized-string path. Worker creation also fails in ways the design does not detect: `new Worker(url)` does not throw on a 404 or a CSP `worker-src` denial — it dispatches an asynchronous `error` event — so "dead worker" detection needs an onerror handler plus a timeout, neither specified; and a worker that starts but thrashes under memory pressure never reports dead at all.
- **Effect**: The design's two memory rulings contradict each other, and the contradiction resolves in the wrong direction precisely under memory pressure: the fallback for a worker that failed *because* memory was scarce is the allocation pattern that needs the most memory. A renderer OOM here destroys the evidence and lands in Q7's no-report death class (no page event, and nothing at all on the Firefox arm).
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — owns both the peak-memory budget and the compression failure path, and the two rulings are stated in adjacent sentences without reconciliation; spike lane exists but tests only the happy shape`
- **Rationale**: Q9's own text carries both claims. The spike list's delivery bullet says "worker-side gzip producing a transferable buffer with the degrade-to-uncompressed path forced" — forcing the degrade path is scoped, but the memory consequence of the degrade path is not what the bullet measures, and the peak-memory bullet in Q9 defers to "a spike measurement (question 15) against intake item f's hardware answer" for the *worker* shape only.

#### delivery-and-transport-16 — Q9 compression — the degrade path's size and headers

- **Mode** *(high)*: Degrading to uncompressed multiplies the POST body by the 5–10× planning ratio, past an endpoint ceiling that was sized on the compressed figure, and the design does not say whether `Content-Encoding` is dropped in lockstep.
- **Mechanism**: Q9 makes gzip conditional on intake c confirming the endpoint decodes it. If it does, the endpoint's accepted maximum will in practice be negotiated against compressed sizes, and the bundle will be sized to fill it — that is what the 5–10× planning ratio is for. The degrade path then sends the same evidence at 5–10× the size. Two further unspecified points: whether the transport still stamps `Content-Encoding: gzip` on a body that is no longer gzipped (which yields a 400 from any correct server), and whether the degrade decision happens before or after the body has been chunked to the worker.
- **Effect**: "Never to silent loss" is only true if the larger body is accepted. With a ceiling, the degrade path converts a compression failure into a guaranteed 413 that (per the missing status classification, above) is then retried 8 times at 5–10× the bandwidth before dropping. A mismatched `Content-Encoding` header converts it into a guaranteed 400 with the same retry waste.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — rules the failure path total-by-construction without reconciling it against the ceiling the same paragraph makes an intake-c input`
- **Rationale**: Q9's budget-one paragraph owns both the ceiling ("the endpoint's actual ceiling is intake item c") and the degrade rule; neither is expressed in terms of the other, and no text anywhere describes shrinking, splitting, or re-chunking a bundle that a receiver rejects on size. The report's own critique of Bugsnag — a 1 MB ceiling that "strips all event metadata client-side ... replacing it with a warning string" — is the field's example of what a designed response to a ceiling looks like; the build has none.

#### delivery-and-transport-17 — Q9 assembly — worker feasibility as a fact

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The design adds a Web Worker to a page whose CSP and build configuration were never checked for one, while the report's own assumption list says no application code runs in workers.
- **Mechanism**: The report checks CSP carefully in one place — fast-redact is disqualified because `Function`-constructor compilation "throws under a CSP without `unsafe-eval` — a hazard to import into a hardened page" — establishing that the page is treated as CSP-hardened. It then specifies a gzip/assembly worker without checking `worker-src`/`child-src`, without checking whether a `blob:` worker URL (the common bundler-free pattern) is permitted, and without checking that Vite 8 emits the worker chunk under the app's build (facts/app-profile.md pins Vite 8.0.16). Intake item k asked whether app code runs in workers; the report's assumption list answers "No application code runs in Web Workers (item k); `record()` is main-thread" — an assumption stated one section after a design that introduces one.
- **Effect**: On a hardened page the worker never starts, so every seal takes the degrade path (above) permanently: main-thread envelope-sized assembly on every trigger, uncompressed bodies against a compressed-sized ceiling, and the peak-memory ruling void for the whole fleet. The failure is silent and fleet-wide, and no intake item would have surfaced it.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: No question owns worker feasibility. Q9 asserts the worker shape as "mechanically right"; the rubric row for Browser compatibility lists floor-checked primitives ("TextEncoder, CompressionStream FF113+, sendBeacon FF31+, BroadcastChannel FF38+") and omits `Worker` and CSP. Intake items a–l contain no CSP question. The report's own assumption line about workers concerns app code, so it does not cover this.

#### delivery-and-transport-18 — Q9 assembly / Q11 seal budget — main-thread time

- **Mode** *(high)*: Per-bucket assembly and `TextEncoder` encoding to transferable buffers is envelope-scale main-thread CPU that no budget covers; only its memory was budgeted.
- **Mechanism**: To hand the worker "bucket-sized chunks, transferable buffers", the main thread must build each bucket's JSON string and encode it to a `Uint8Array` — strings are not transferable, which the report states as the reason for the shape. That is a full serialize-plus-encode of the entire retained envelope (up to 50 MB) on the main thread, in per-bucket steps, per seal. Q9 budgets only the memory ("capping the peak at roughly envelope + largest-bucket"); Q11's seal bullet is also memory-shaped ("the seal path allocates only pointer copies plus the per-bucket chunk in flight"). Nothing says the chunks are yielded across macrotasks, and the interface only guarantees that `capture()` is synchronous *through the seal*, leaving assembly's scheduling unspecified — including whether the recorder calls `transport(bundle)` synchronously from inside `capture()`, in which case any synchronous work the app transport does before its first `await` runs on the trigger turn (which may be inside `window.onerror`).
- **Effect**: Hundreds of milliseconds to seconds of main-thread jank per trigger on embedded-class hardware (the report's own 10× de-rate), at the moment the app is already failing — the "recorder becomes the incident" outcome, arriving through the collection step rather than the capture step. Under the delivery-driven trigger mill (above) it repeats every cooldown window.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — budgets peak memory at seal and names the spike for it; partial:Q11 — lists the seal as a hazard but prices it in allocations; spike list omits assembly latency`
- **Rationale**: Q15's delivery bullet measures "worker-side gzip producing a transferable buffer" and the projection bullet measures per-record capture cost inside the dispatch path. Neither measures the main-thread cost of assembling and encoding a full envelope. The plan-level pass's trigger-time-memory-spike finding was dispositioned into Q9's peak-memory sub-question and Q11's hazard list — both memory-framed — so the latency dimension was never brought in.

#### delivery-and-transport-19 — Q16 × Q10 — delivery under N tabs

- **Mode** *(high)*: N tabs run N independent delivery machines: N × (one in-flight + one pending) envelope-scale bundles resident, and N phase-aligned retry schedules against the same endpoint.
- **Mechanism**: Q16 rules per-tab recorders with no coordination and states the flagged cost as capture-side: "The envelope multiplication (N × 10–50 MB) is flagged to intake item j". The delivery slots are additional to that: each tab's transport holds up to two sealed bundles (mode above), so the true multiplier is N × up to 3× envelope. And because one device-level cause fires all N tabs' nets within the same instant, their retry schedules start together; per-bundle jitter decorrelates attempts within a tab but not the initial burst across tabs.
- **Effect**: At N=2 on a 50 MB envelope the recorder's resident footprint approaches 300 MB on a device whose OS runs from RAM — an OS-level failure mode caused by the diagnostics tool — and the endpoint sees a synchronized N-way upload of near-duplicate multi-MB bundles. Q16's claim that the design is "correct at any N, merely duplicative" holds for identity and cooldown semantics and does not hold for memory or endpoint load.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q16 — flags the capture-envelope multiplication to intake item j and does not carry the delivery slots or the retry burst into that flag`
- **Rationale**: Q16's cost accounting is explicit and enumerates: per-tab rings, N nets, N near-duplicate bundles, N × envelope of device RAM. The pending/in-flight bundle residency is a Q10 construct introduced in a different section, and Q10's memory argument ("the pinned bundle is 10–50 MB of RAM on a RAM-disk device") is written single-tab. The two sections' numbers were never composed.

#### delivery-and-transport-20 — Q7 cooldown × Q10 delivery health

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Nothing throttles sealing when delivery is known-dead, so a known-undeliverable recorder keeps paying the full seal + assembly + gzip + POST cost every cooldown window.
- **Mechanism**: Q7's cooldown is time-based only: "one bundle per cooldown window (config; the interface's default is 30 s)". The delivery loop's knowledge that the endpoint has failed 8 times in a row never reaches the trigger path — the trigger path is inside the recorder, the delivery knowledge is behind the `transport` seam, and the seam is one-directional by design (`(bundle) => void | Promise<void>`; "outcomes are health counters, never caller-branchable" is the panel's ruling for `record()`, and the same shape is applied to delivery). So with the endpoint down and an error loop running, the recorder seals twice a minute forever.
- **Effect**: Sustained, self-inflicted CPU and allocation load with a zero-percent chance of delivering anything, on a degraded device, for as long as the outage lasts. Every one of those bundles is also a candidate for the evict-older-pending churn, so the work produces neither evidence nor a counter that survives.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q7 owns cooldown and dedup and frames both as trigger-storm protection ("an error loop shows up as `triggers.suppressed: 4000`, not as 4000 bundles") — a bound on bundle *count* per unit time, not a feedback path from delivery health. Q10 owns delivery and never proposes signalling upstream. Q11's kill switch is construction-time by ruling and cannot be used mid-session. The one-directional seam is a deliberate design choice, which is what makes this a mode the design creates.

#### delivery-and-transport-21 — Q9 bundle assembly × Q10 delivery independence

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The transport adapter reads live `IngressStats` off the boundary at assembly time, coupling report assembly to the very subsystem the report may be about.
- **Mechanism**: Q9: "0070's six `IngressStats` counters and wire-2 depths ride the wire payload too, but outside the recorder's surface: the transport adapter — app code assembling the OpenAPI wire body from the `SealedBundle` — reads them at assembly time." Assembly runs after the seal, asynchronously. If the incident is a boundary failure, the composition root's recovery path is (per Q6's worked example) to dispose and recreate the boundary; an adapter holding the old `boundary.actor` reads from a disposed instance, or throws. That throw is inside app transport code, which lands in the unhandled-rejection path (above) and, per I1, is not the recorder's to catch.
- **Effect**: Q10's headline claim — "the recorder must not depend on the surface it is diagnosing" — is true of the POST carrier and false of the body assembly. A boundary-shaped incident can prevent its own report from being assembled, or produce a bundle whose environment block reads a recreated boundary's zeroed counters and reports them as the incident's state (the "own read clock" stamp makes the timing honest but does not make the values relevant).
- **Owner**: `partial:Q10 — asserts delivery independence for the carrier only; partial:Q9 — chooses the assembly-time read explicitly to keep the recorder observation-only, without pricing the coupling it creates`
- **Rationale**: Both texts are explicit and each is locally sound: Q9 moves the stats read out of the recorder to preserve I6; Q10 keeps the POST off the boundary to preserve independence. The composition puts a live-boundary dependency back into the delivery path one layer up, and no question owns the composed property.

#### delivery-and-transport-22 — I6 / Q11 — self-capture defence

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Self-capture is prevented by two statements of the same rule with no detection: routing the transport through the app's fetcher breaks I6 and the `x-recorder-request` filter simultaneously, and the resulting evidence loss is indistinguishable from ordinary ring pressure.
- **Mechanism**: I6 says the injected transport "MUST NOT route through any fetcher a tap wraps"; Q11's first cut says "the HTTP wrap skips requests carrying the recorder's own marker header (the wrap adds `x-recorder-request: 1` to its POST and filters on it)". Both depend on the same app-owned transport implementation. Under heavy agent-authored churn the obvious refactor — route the report POST through the boundary fetcher, which already has base URL, auth, and retry — satisfies the type, compiles, and defeats both defences at once. There is no runtime check (a URL/marker assertion in the http tap, a counter for self-shaped records) and no health field that would show it.
- **Effect**: Every retry of a multi-MB report POST becomes an entry in the http bucket, evicting real HTTP history under byte accounting (the 8 MB http bucket in the worked defaults is filled by one bundle POST), and bundle N+1 contains bundle N's body truncated at `maxEntryBytes`. The evictions increment `evicted`, so I4's ledger reconciles perfectly and the loss reads as normal traffic pressure — well-formed wrong evidence, the exact anti-pattern RD-5 exists to prevent, at the delivery seam.
- **Owner**: `partial:I6 — states the rule as a MUST NOT on injected code; partial:Q11 — supplies a filter that depends on the same code honouring the rule`
- **Rationale**: The plan-level pass dispositioned "Recorder's own report POST captured by the HTTP bucket" as Q11-owned, noting that "whether it is excluded automatically depends on Q10's carrier choice — a bare fetch outside the boundary escapes the wrap for free". The design takes the bare-fetch route, which is the right answer, and then leaves it as prose plus a redundant filter. Nothing detects the violation, and the failure is silent by construction because the ledger stays consistent.

#### delivery-and-transport-23 — Q10 slot policy — victim selection

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Evict-older-pending, examined against a flapping endpoint, deterministically discards the middle of an incident: the slot policy privileges the first bundle's five-minute backoff schedule over every subsequent piece of evidence.
- **Mechanism**: Q10: "one bundle in flight at a time plus **at most one pending** — a third seal arriving while both slots are full evicts the older pending bundle". The in-flight slot is held for the whole retry schedule, including the long backoff waits that dominate an 8-attempt/5-minute horizon. With a 30 s cooldown, roughly ten seals occur during one horizon: the first holds the in-flight slot; the pending slot always holds the newest; the eight in between are evicted. (Note the brief's premise inverts: the *first* incident is the one the policy protects; the middle is the victim. After exhaustion the first is dropped and the newest is promoted, so the survivors are the first — if the endpoint recovers inside five minutes — and the last.)
- **Effect**: For cascade failures, where the diagnostic value is in the progression rather than the endpoints, the recorder reliably keeps the two least informative samples. The report's stated rationale for the policy — "so sealed evidence can never accumulate unboundedly behind a slow endpoint" — argues for *a* bound and does not argue for this victim; alternatives (evict-newest-refused with a counted refusal, or demoting an in-flight bundle that is merely waiting out a backoff so a newer one can probe a recovered endpoint) are not weighed.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q10 — rules the bound and counts the drop; the choice of victim is asserted with a rationale that only justifies bounding`
- **Rationale**: Q10's sentence contains the entire policy and its justification. The report elsewhere reasons carefully about victim selection when it matters — Q2 rejects cross-bucket evict-oldest precisely because "cross-bucket evict-oldest deterministically starves quiet buckets ... handing the noisy bucket the space the rare context needed" — so the omission at the delivery slot is inconsistent with the report's own standard, and it is the report that introduces the second slot.

#### delivery-and-transport-24 — Q10 auth posture / Q9 budget two

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Credential expiry or a header-based auth scheme silently disables both delivery paths, and `sendBeacon` cannot carry an `Authorization` header at all.
- **Mechanism**: Q10: "the design assumes same-origin-or-CORS-with-credentials" — i.e. cookie credentials. Cookies expire, and on a long-lived kiosk session (fleet devices, single-app) expiry mid-session is the normal case, not the exception. Every POST then 401s; with no permanent/transient classification each bundle burns 8 attempts before dropping; and there is no re-auth or refresh hook in the transport slot or anywhere in `RecorderConfig`. Separately, if intake c answers that the endpoint requires a bearer token, the beacon path cannot authenticate at all — `navigator.sendBeacon` accepts no headers — so the loss marker 401s for the whole fleet, unobservably (opaque no-cors response).
- **Effect**: Total delivery loss from an ordinary session-lifetime event, with the same silence as the other delivery failures: no counter carrier, no marker (or a marker that is itself rejected), no health field. If the answer to intake c is header auth, the entire budget-two design needs a backend change (cookie or URL token) that the survey has not priced.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q10 — defers auth to intake item c and states an assumption, without naming the sendBeacon-cannot-set-headers constraint that invalidates budget two independently of how item c answers`
- **Rationale**: The plan-level pass raised exactly this as "Auth cannot ride the last-gasp sender" (partial:Q10, "the missing piece is a design constraint, not a fact, so the intake item alone does not cover it"). The design's Q9 budget-two paragraph reasons about content type and quota in detail and never mentions authentication; Q10's one clause repeats the deferral. The plan-level disposition is unchanged by the design.

#### delivery-and-transport-25 — Q6 lifecycle — dispose() versus transport-owned listeners

- **Mode** *(low)*: `dispose()` disarms only the nets the recorder armed, so the beacon listener the delivery layer registers survives disposal and keeps firing.
- **Mechanism**: The interface: "dispose(): Composition-root lifecycle only (tests, HMR): disarms the nets it armed, frees rings." The `visibilitychange`/`pagehide` listener that sends the loss marker is registered behind the `transport` seam (Q9/Q10 put all delivery machinery there), so it is not a net the recorder armed and `dispose()` has no handle on it. Under HMR — the named use case — successive recorder instances each leave a listener and a delivery loop behind.
- **Effect**: After disposal or across HMR reloads, orphaned listeners fire loss markers referencing dead bundles and consume the shared 64 KiB beacon budget; in tests, a disposed recorder still emits network traffic, which makes the delivery lane of the spike harder to isolate than the report assumes. Minor in production, but it is a lifecycle hole the interface claims to close.
- **Owner**: `partial:I9/dispose — lifecycle is defined over nets and rings, and the delivery layer is deliberately outside the interface, so nothing joins the two`
- **Rationale**: The `dispose()` doc comment enumerates its scope exactly. Q9 and Q10 place the beacon and the retry loop behind the transport seam. Because the seam is opaque by design ("the interface cannot tell" what is behind it), no recorder-side lifecycle call can reach them — the same opacity that makes mode 'delivery policy lives in app code' unenforceable makes teardown unreachable.

### Config, construction, and tap lifecycle (25 modes)

#### config-and-lifecycle-1 — I8 fail-safe-loud / table-level validation (report Q2 bullet 4, Recommendation risk 6)

- **Mode** *(high)*: A table-level config error makes the recorder inert, which destroys the only off-device channel that would report it.
- **Mechanism**: Q2 rules that a config whose Σ maxBytes exceeds the envelope is "a table-level validation error ... so the recorder comes up disabled-loud (configErrors populated, every entry point inert)". Inert entry points include capture(), so no seal ever happens, so no SealedBundle ever reaches transport, so BundleHeader.configErrors — named as the loud channel in I8 ("every rejection appears in health().configErrors and in every bundle header") — is never transmitted. The residue is a console line in a kiosk browser and a health() nobody calls.
- **Effect**: The worst-graded misconfiguration (recorder fully dead fleet-wide) is exactly the one that guarantees zero telemetry about itself; the team learns at the first incident that needed a bundle, which is the plan-level 'recorder never initialized' failure re-created by the design's own escalation rule.
- **Owner**: `partial:I8 — the two loud channels are health() (pull-only, in-page) and the bundle header (unreachable when the recorder is disabled)`
- **Rationale**: I8 states "'Silent' is averted by the ledger and the one-line startup assertion". Neither averts this case: the ledger (I4) is per-bucket DropCounters that only exist for buckets that exist, and a disabled recorder has no ledger to ship; the assertion is console-only (see next mode). The design text nowhere provides an out-of-band or fail-loud-at-delivery path for a recorder that comes up disabled.

#### config-and-lifecycle-2 — I8 startup assertion (worked example line: console.assert(recorder.health().configErrors.length === 0, ...))

- **Mode** *(high)*: The "one-line startup assertion" that carries the whole 'loud' half of fail-safe-loud is unenforceable integrator code emitting a signal nobody on this fleet can hear.
- **Mechanism**: Three compounding facts: (1) it lives in the app's composition root, not behind the interface — nothing in createFlightRecorder can require it, and under "heavy agent-authored churn" a composition-root rewrite silently drops it; (2) console.assert neither throws nor changes control flow, and the fleet's devices are RAM-disk appliances whose console no one opens; (3) the app builds with Vite 8, where production console-stripping (esbuild `drop: ['console']` / pure-annotation minification) is a routine setting that deletes the line from the shipped bundle entirely. The interface has no health-change callback, no onConfigError hook, and no way to observe that the guard is absent.
- **Effect**: In production the design's stated defence against I8 hiding a typo'd config in the fleet does not exist; a mistyped bucket row disables a bucket for the fleet's lifetime with no observable consequence until a bundle happens to arrive carrying configErrors — and per the previous mode, the highest-severity class of error prevents that arrival.
- **Owner**: `partial:I8 — I8 names the assertion as the mechanism that averts silence but specifies no enforcement, no non-console channel, and no build-mode caveat`
- **Rationale**: I8: "'Silent' is averted by the ledger and the one-line startup assertion (health().configErrors.length === 0), not by throwing." The health() doc repeats it: "The startup guard against I8 hiding a typo'd config in the fleet is one line". The design's own refusal of fail-fast construction rests on this guard doing the work; the guard is the weakest possible carrier for that load on a headless fleet.

#### config-and-lifecycle-3 — RecorderConfig.buckets validation / attach() / RecorderHealth

- **Mode** *(medium)*: A bucket disabled by row-level validation is indistinguishable from a bucket that was never declared, in every surface the design exposes.
- **Mechanism**: I8 says "An invalid bucket row disables that bucket"; attach() says an "Unknown bucket name (the expected typo under loose TS)" yields "an inert tap plus a counted config error". Both paths produce: an inert RecorderTap, a string in configErrors, and — for the row-invalid case — an unspecified presence in health().buckets and in SealedBundle.buckets. Nothing in BucketHealth or SealedBucket carries a 'declared but rejected' state, and unknownBuckets is keyed by name, so a declared-but-rejected 'mqtt' plausibly appears there too, reading as a typo of a name that was in fact spelled correctly.
- **Effect**: The bundle reader cannot separate "this bucket's row was malformed" from "this bucket was never configured" from "someone typo'd the name at the attach site"; the operator response differs in each case, and the design's own doctrine ("two spellings of absence is one too many") is violated by its own error taxonomy.
- **Owner**: `partial:I8 — the row-invalid outcome is named but its representation in health().buckets, unknownBuckets, and SealedBundle.buckets is never specified`
- **Rationale**: BucketHealth's fields are attachedSince/detachedAt/detachReason/attachEpoch/lastRecordAt/seq/count/bytes/drops — none expresses 'rejected at validation'. SealedBundle.buckets is Record<string, SealedBucket> with no membership rule stated for disabled rows. The refusal of per-bucket enabled flags (Q11: "a disabled bucket is an undeclared bucket") deliberately collapses these states, which is defensible for a deliberate omission but not for a validation failure.

#### config-and-lifecycle-4 — RecorderHealth.unknownBuckets / configErrors (adopted from the registry variant)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The per-name unknown-bucket ledger and configErrors are unbounded, caller-keyed allocations that sit outside the byte meter and ride every bundle header.
- **Mechanism**: unknownBuckets is "record() traffic to undeclared bucket names, keyed by the offending name" with "each name also lands in configErrors once"; configErrors is a readonly string[]. No cap is stated for either. The design actively invites dynamic names — "Custom buckets attach through the same interface the built-ins use" — so an agent-authored per-entity pattern (attach(`sensor-${id}`) over a device roster, or a name built from a topic segment) creates one map entry, one configErrors string, and one RecorderTap object per distinct name, forever. I3 bounds "Per bucket, count ≤ maxCount and bytes ≤ maxBytes"; a name that names no bucket is bounded by nothing.
