- **Effect**: An uncapped memory growth path inside the component whose entire thesis is bounded-by-construction, on a RAM-disk device where a full RAM disk is an OS-level failure — plus per-bundle header inflation that can push the payload past the endpoint's (unknown, intake c) size ceiling. I4's ledger cannot account for it: these are not records, so nothing counts them as loss.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: I3's bound is scoped "Per bucket"; the ledger is by construction not a bucket. I4 reconciles recorded/retained/evicted/oversized/projectionFailures — all record-shaped. The panel adopted the per-name ledger explicitly ("an aggregate count hides which name was wrong") without a sizing rule, while the same question's judge refused a bounded-correlation-set sizing rule only by naming it as still-open ("the bounded correlation open-set needs a sizing rule") — the unknown-bucket ledger got neither a bound nor an open-question flag.

#### config-and-lifecycle-5 — BucketPolicy.project — the one behaviour a row may carry

- **Mode** *(high)*: project() is an unvalidatable callback running inside the host's latency-disciplined dispatch turn, and neither of its two worst behaviours — mutating the caller's live entry, and taking too long — is covered by any invariant.
- **Mechanism**: The validator can assert `typeof project === 'function'` and nothing else. Two escapes follow. (a) Mutation: the mqtt tap hands project() the live wire-1 payload (0060 specifies no freeze/clone); a project that sorts an array in place, deletes a key, or normalizes a field mutates the object that sibling listeners in the same dispatch turn read afterwards — the recorder altering the app's data, the exact inverse of Q3's accepted 'sibling mutates our view'. I2 constrains only what the recorder *retains* ("holds no reference to the caller's entry"); I6's "Observation only" enumerates subscribing, publishing, classifying, transacting, self-reporting — not mutating. (b) Latency: I1 converts a *throw* to a counted drop, but a project() that spins (an accidental O(n²) over a 10k-element array, a regex backtrack, an await-less busy loop) cannot be caught; Q11's self-protective detach fires on a `tapError` counter, i.e. on throws, so a slow project has no counter, no threshold, and no detach.
- **Effect**: (a) turns the recorder into a source of app data corruption for every other wire-1 consumer; (b) is the plan's headline hazard — a slow listener pushes 0060's shared dispatch queue (bound 256, shed-oldest) into shedding for every consumer — reachable purely through a config row that the validator passed.
- **Owner**: `partial:I1/Q11 — I1 covers throws only; Q11's per-tap containment and self-protective detach are error-counted, so the latency and mutation escapes are unowned`
- **Rationale**: BucketPolicy.project: "Runs app code and may throw: a throw is a counted projectionFailure" — throw is the only failure mode contemplated. Q11: "every tap body is bounded — project, meter, push, return" is asserted of the recorder's own code and cannot bind a config-supplied function. The spike list verifies "a scrubber that throws mid-walk" — again throws only.

#### config-and-lifecycle-6 — RecorderConfig.transport — the delivery seam

- **Mode** *(high)*: A mis-wired transport produces a `{outcome: 'sealed'}` receipt and total, permanently silent evidence loss, because every channel that would report the failure runs through the same broken function.
- **Mechanism**: transport is `(bundle) => void | Promise<void>`, validatable only as a typeof check. If it is undefined, not a function, or throws synchronously (a closure over a startup-config field that resolved late — see the async-config mode), I1 requires the recorder to swallow it ("capture() ... never throw into the caller"). CaptureReceipt has exactly four outcomes — sealed, coalesced, suppressed, disabled — none of which can express 'sealed but undeliverable', so the caller receives a success receipt. Q10's compensating counter ("the drop increments deliveryFailed, and the counter rides the next bundle and the last-gasp loss marker") is unreachable: the next bundle also goes through transport, and the last-gasp loss marker is itself emitted behind the transport seam (the interface exposes no separate beacon member).
- **Effect**: The recorder appears healthy in every in-page surface — health().triggers.sealed increments, drop counters stay clean, configErrors is empty — while 100% of evidence is destroyed; the backend sees a device that never reports, which its absence-based detection reads as a crashed device.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q10 — Q10 owns delivery policy and names deliveryFailed, but both of its visibility channels are downstream of the failed component, and CaptureReceipt has no undeliverable outcome`
- **Rationale**: Q10: "All of this lives behind question 6's `transport` seam — it is delivery policy, invisible to the capture surface." That invisibility is exactly the defect here: the capture surface is the only surface the app can observe synchronously, and it is contractually forbidden from telling the truth about delivery. I8's validation story covers bucket rows and the envelope sum; it never addresses non-bucket config fields.

#### config-and-lifecycle-7 — Config validator / I3 static envelope check (BucketPolicy.maxBytes, maxCount, maxEntryBytes)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The envelope invariant is a single sum test, so out-of-domain numbers pass it — and a negative maxBytes both silently zeroes its own bucket and buys headroom for an over-budget sibling.
- **Mechanism**: I3 specifies exactly one check: "Σ maxBytes + header allowance ≤ envelope". Under "very loose" TS with agent-authored config, the runtime values are whatever arrived. `{mqtt: 40*MiB, scratch: -30*MiB}` sums to 10 MiB and passes, while the mqtt ring holds 40 MiB — the envelope invariant is defeated by arithmetic, not by eviction. A bucket with a negative maxBytes evicts to empty on every push and then counts the record oversized, so it reports plausible-looking counters while retaining nothing. maxBytes: NaN makes every `bytes > maxBytes` comparison false — eviction never fires, the ring is unbounded — and would only be caught if the validator treats a NaN sum as a failure, which "Σ ≤ envelope" does by accident rather than by rule. maxEntryBytes: NaN disables truncation, letting a multi-MB projection materialize on the dispatch turn.
- **Effect**: The design's first-order safety property — bounded by construction, in bytes, on a device where RAM exhaustion is an OS-level failure — is defeated by config values the validator was specified to accept, and two of the three variants leave the health block reading normal.
- **Owner**: `partial:I3 — the invariant states a sum relation and no per-field domain (finite, positive, integer, maxEntryBytes ≤ maxBytes) check`
- **Rationale**: I3 verbatim: "The global 10–50 MB envelope is enforced STATICALLY by the config validator (Σ maxBytes + header allowance ≤ envelope)". Q2 repeats it as the answer to the starvation finding. Neither text, nor BucketPolicy's field comments, states a domain constraint on any numeric field; the plan-level pass only reached "a bucket declared with no bound" (missing field), not an actively hostile value that passes the sum.

#### config-and-lifecycle-8 — BucketPolicy.maxEntryBytes default vs maxBytes/maxCount coherence

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The stated default `min(maxBytes / 8, 256 KiB)` and the unconstrained maxCount/maxBytes pairing let a config declare a history depth it can never hold, with no validation error and no runtime signal.
- **Mechanism**: Two concrete cases. (a) Small bucket: a custom bucket with maxBytes 32 KiB gets maxEntryBytes 4 KiB by default, so every record over 4 KiB is truncated-with-copy and marked — the bucket 'works', its counters increment, and every record in it is a stump. Push maxBytes to 8 KiB and the default is 1 KiB. (b) Ratio incoherence: the worked example declares `xstate: { maxCount: 10_000, maxBytes: 8 * MiB }`, i.e. an implied mean record of 838 B. xstate microstep events carrying snapshots routinely project larger, so the byte cap binds first and the real depth is a few hundred — the declared 10,000 is decorative. The validator checks neither ratio, and BucketHealth exposes count and bytes but no 'effective depth' or 'binding cap' signal.
- **Effect**: Config seeds (intake item d) will be written against the declared numbers; the bundle silently delivers a fraction of the history the team believes it configured, and the discrepancy is only derivable by dividing two health fields after the fact.
- **Owner**: `partial:I3 — the sum invariant is the only cross-field rule; no coherence check on maxEntryBytes/maxBytes/maxCount exists, and no health field names which cap is binding`
- **Rationale**: BucketPolicy: "Default min(maxBytes / 8, 256 KiB)" is stated without a floor or a warning threshold; "An entry whose truncation floor still exceeds maxBytes is dropped and counted oversized" handles only the extreme. Q2's per-entry-overhead bullet declares index overhead "as a stated constant multiplied by maxCount" — showing the design does reason about maxCount arithmetic, but only for the overhead term, never for whether maxCount is reachable at all.

#### config-and-lifecycle-9 — RedactionRules.patterns: RegExp[]

- **Mode** *(high)*: Accepting live RegExp objects in config admits lastIndex statefulness: a global/sticky pattern either misses every other record (PII leaks) or throws on every record (all buckets silently empty), depending on whether the config was frozen.
- **Mechanism**: `RedactionRules { keys?, paths?, patterns?: RegExp[] }` takes caller-constructed RegExp objects, reused across every projection at ~50/s. (a) Unfrozen: `/token=\w+/g` used via .test()/.exec() advances lastIndex and returns false on the next call from a non-zero offset, so redaction succeeds on record 1, fails on record 2, succeeds on record 3 — an alternating leak with no marker, since RD-10's `{redacted, ruleId}` is stamped only when a rule fires. (b) Frozen: the design says the config is "validated and FROZEN at construction", and the natural implementation of that (Object.freeze over the config object graph) makes lastIndex non-writable; RegExpBuiltinExec performs Set(lastIndex, throw=true) whenever global or sticky is set, so exec/test throws TypeError. Under I1 that throw becomes a counted projectionFailure on every single record — every bucket empties while projectionFailures climbs.
- **Effect**: Branch (a) exfiltrates exactly the data intake item b exists to protect, in a pattern that looks like the rule 'mostly works'. Branch (b) is a total capture blackout whose only evidence is a counter inside a bundle that now contains no records — and which the reader will read as 'the app was idle'.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q13 rules that the rules are "fast-redact-style field/path/pattern data" with an owned applier and that markers distinguish redaction from truncation; nothing in Q13, I8, or RedactionRules constrains regex flags, requires per-use cloning or lastIndex reset, or contemplates the frozen-regex interaction that the config's own freeze ruling creates. The design's freeze language and its RegExp field are two independently reasonable choices that combine into a defect.

#### config-and-lifecycle-10 — RedactionRules.keys/paths — validator coverage

- **Mode** *(high)*: Key and path rules cannot be validated against record shapes the validator has never seen, and a rule that matches nothing is indistinguishable from a rule that had nothing to match.
- **Mechanism**: The worked example ships `paths: ['payload.operator.*']`. The validator can check that the string parses as a path expression; it cannot know that the projected mqtt record nests the payload one level deeper (`{channel, topic, params, payload}` — so the real path is `payload.operator.*` only if the tap's own wrapper shape is stable, which it is not: the tap builds that literal in app code, and Q4 says custom buckets and future taps choose their own shapes). A one-character path typo, a bucket whose project() reshapes the record after the paths were written, or a tap-side field rename all produce a rule that silently matches zero nodes. RD-10 stamps `{redacted, ruleId}` only on application, so the bundle carries no evidence that a rule existed and never fired.
- **Effect**: PII ships to the report endpoint with a bundle that looks correctly redacted; the failure is invisible at every layer, and it is created by exactly the churn the app profile predicts (agent-authored renames across two files that the type system does not connect).
- **Owner**: `partial:Q13 — Q13 owns rule provenance, application point, and markers, but names no coverage assertion (a rule that never fires, a ruleId absent from every bundle) and no shape contract between a tap's record literal and the path rules written against it`
- **Rationale**: Q13: "they apply per bucket at projection time ... every application stamps `{redacted, ruleId}`" — application-time evidence only. Q6's RedactionRules comment claims "path rules provide per-bucket scoping where needed", which makes the path strings load-bearing for scoping as well as matching, doubling the blast radius of a stale path. The spike list (Q15) tests projection, boundedness, seal, containment, delivery, schema validity — no redaction-coverage property.

#### config-and-lifecycle-11 — BundleHeader.configDigest (Q9's replacement for the raw config snapshot)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: A digest computed over a config containing functions and RegExps is blind to precisely the fields the validator is also blind to, so two materially different capture regimes attest identically.
- **Mechanism**: configDigest is "Digest, not the raw config — plus the I8 residue, so a thinned config is visible." Any JSON-based digest drops `project` and `transport` (functions serialize to nothing) and renders each RegExp in `redaction.patterns` as `{}`. So a build that changed a bucket's project() to a different scrubber, or swapped the redaction pattern set, or replaced the transport, produces a byte-identical digest to the previous build. The digest's one job — letting the backend resolve which config produced a bundle — silently fails for the three fields with the largest effect on bundle content, and configErrors (the 'residue') is empty in all these cases because they are valid configs.
- **Effect**: Post-incident analysis attributes a bundle to a config that did not produce it; a regression introduced by an agent-edited project() or a narrowed redaction rule set is undetectable from the archive, and the fleet-wide question 'which devices are running the old scrubber?' has no answer.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — Q9 chose the digest to dissolve the secrets-in-snapshot hazard and priced the attribution benefit, but not the callback/RegExp blindness`
- **Rationale**: Q9: "the digest satisfies RD-7's attribution need (the resolvable config lives in deployment)" — attribution presumes the digest distinguishes deployments, which it does not across function or RegExp changes. RD-7 reserves "config snapshot" as a cheap-now/unrecoverable-later field, so the weakening is exactly the foreclosure class question 12 exists to prevent.

#### config-and-lifecycle-12 — RecorderConfig.identity → BundleHeader (deviceId, appBuild) vs the whole-bundle redaction ruling

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Identity fields bypass the seal's redaction pass, which is enumerated as covering three specific bypass sites and not a fourth.
- **Mechanism**: Q6's redaction comment names the seal's extra coverage precisely: "AND, at seal, the three fields that bypass them: trigger error/stack, caller context, config snapshot", and Q13 repeats the same three. BundleHeader.deviceId and appBuild are neither bucket-projected nor in that list, so they leave the device unfiltered by construction. The assumed source of deviceId is the MQTT clientId (Q9: "the MQTT clientId is the candidate — device-scoped on a static roster"), i.e. a persistent, device-identifying string, shipped before intake item b ("what may leave the device") has answered — while the same report's conservative default redacts ordinary payload leaf values to structural skeletons.
- **Effect**: The design's most conservative posture applies to payload bodies while a stable device identifier rides every header unredacted and unmarked; if item b comes back restricting device-level identifiers, the fix is a schema change to a field the dedup key and the absence-detection query both depend on.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Both Q6's interface comment and Q13's prose enumerate the seal's bypass set exhaustively as three fields. Q9 introduces the identity block after that enumeration was written and never revisits it. No invariant covers header identity, and RedactionRules has no path expression that could reach header fields (paths are documented as per-bucket scoping).

#### config-and-lifecycle-13 — Identity generation behind the seam (pageIncarnationId, bundleId)

- **Mode** *(high)*: Id minting is unspecified as to primitive and secure-context requirement; on a non-HTTPS origin `crypto.randomUUID` does not exist, and I8 forbids the resulting failure from being loud.
- **Mechanism**: Q9 specifies "pageIncarnationId (a UUID minted once per page load)" and RecorderConfig says "pageIncarnationId and per-capture bundleId are generated behind the seam". `crypto.randomUUID()` is secure-context-only (Firefox 95+, Chrome 92+, but undefined on an http:// origin — unlike `crypto.getRandomValues`, which is not restricted). A LAN-served kiosk fleet on http:// with a wss:// broker connection is entirely consistent with every fact in the app profile. Calling an undefined member throws TypeError at construction, and I8 requires createFlightRecorder to swallow it ("NEVER throws"). The recorder then either comes up disabled (no bundle can report why — see mode 1) or ships bundles missing `pageIncarnationId`, which BundleHeader declares required (`readonly pageIncarnationId: string`) and the vendored OpenAPI contract will therefore mark required — so every POST is 400'd, retried 8 times, and dropped by Q10's bounded-then-drop rule.
- **Effect**: A single origin-scheme fact turns the entire recorder into a silent no-op fleet-wide, with the backend seeing a device that never reports (which its absence-based crash detection reads as a crashed device — a false positive on every device, permanently).
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: The rubric's Browser compatibility row claims "every primitive floor-checked this survey (TextEncoder, CompressionStream FF113+, sendBeacon FF31+, BroadcastChannel FF38+)" — the id primitive is absent from that list, and no question states the secure-context requirement or names a fallback (getRandomValues-based id, or a caller-supplied id seed). I8's never-throw rule guarantees the failure is quiet; no invariant covers construction-time capability failures.

#### config-and-lifecycle-14 — RecorderConfig.identity.deviceId?: string (optional)

- **Mode** *(high)*: deviceId is optional, so its absence is not a validation error, and the design accepts a permanently degraded identity mode with no configError, no marker, and no health signal.
- **Mechanism**: `identity: { appBuild: string; deviceId?: string }` with the comment "whether deviceId exists for page code is intake item i". An absent optional passes I8's validator by definition. Downstream, Q9 states the backend "upserts on `(deviceId, pageIncarnationId, bundleId)`" and Q7's Firefox-arm compensation is "absence-based detection at the backend: a device whose bundles and heartbeats stop arriving mid-session is the crash signal, keyed on question 9's device and page-incarnation identity". With deviceId undefined both degrade silently: at-least-once dedup still works per bundle but cross-bundle and cross-tab joins do not, and absence detection — the *only* compensation for the crash-shaped deaths on the Firefox floor arm, which is the fleet's arm — has nothing to key on.
- **Effect**: The recorder runs looking healthy while the two backend capabilities the report leans on hardest are inoperative; and because the same field is optional in the schema, the backend cannot tell 'this fleet has no device identity' from 'this one bundle lost it'.
- **Owner**: `partial:I8 — fail-safe-loud's granularity is row/table validity; a valid-but-crippling omission has no representation, and no invariant requires the identity block to be complete or to record its own degradation`
- **Rationale**: Q9 and Q7 both make deviceId load-bearing; RecorderConfig makes it optional and defers to intake item i; the Assumptions list states "The MQTT clientId is readable by page code and permitted in reports (item i)" as an assumption. Nothing converts a falsified assumption into a visible state — the design would run in the degraded mode without ever declaring it, which is the class of silence I8 was written to prevent.

#### config-and-lifecycle-15 — createFlightRecorder / I3 envelope scope

- **Mode** *(high)*: Nothing in the design binds the number of recorder instances per page, so I3's envelope invariant is per-instance and a second construction doubles the fleet's memory ceiling.
- **Mechanism**: createFlightRecorder is a plain factory with no singleton guard, no registry, and no duplicate detection. Two constructions are easy to reach in this codebase: an agent adding a second composition root or a lazily-imported feature module that builds its own recorder; a test helper imported into production code (the pseudo-monorepo has "no per-directory dependency lists" and only path-based lint between directories); or the HMR path below. Each instance validates its own Σ maxBytes ≤ envelope and each allocates its own rings, so two valid recorders hold 2× the charter envelope — 20–100 MB — on a device whose OS runs from a RAM disk. Both also arm the configured nets, so every error produces two capture() calls whose cooldowns cannot see each other.
- **Effect**: The design's headline safety property is stated as a per-recorder invariant and read by everyone as a per-device budget; the gap is the difference between 50 MB and an unbounded multiple of it, on the one resource whose exhaustion is an OS-level failure.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: I3 is scoped "Per bucket ... The global 10–50 MB envelope is enforced STATICALLY by the config validator" — 'global' means global to one config object. Q16 reasons about N tabs multiplying the envelope and flags it to intake item j, but explicitly frames the multiplier as tabs; N recorders *within* one page is never considered, and the interface offers no way for a second instance to discover the first.

#### config-and-lifecycle-16 — dispose() under Vite HMR (the design names HMR as dispose's use case)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: HMR re-evaluation without an explicit dispose hook leaves the previous recorder alive with its nets armed and its rings resident, and pageIncarnationId turns out to be per-instance rather than per page load.
- **Mechanism**: The app builds with Vite 8 + @vitejs/plugin-react 6, so a module holding `const recorder = createFlightRecorder(...)` re-evaluates on every hot update. Unless the integrator writes `import.meta.hot.dispose(() => recorder.dispose())` — which the design mentions only as a parenthetical use case ("Composition-root lifecycle only (tests, HMR)") and never as a requirement — the old instance's window error/unhandledrejection listeners stay registered and its rings (up to the full envelope) stay reachable through those closures. Result per hot reload: one more armed net set, one more envelope leaked, and one more independent cooldown. Separately, because the id is minted at construction, a page load that hot-reloads five times emits bundles under six different pageIncarnationIds, contradicting Q9's "minted once per page load" and breaking the backend's per-incarnation grouping in exactly the environment where the recorder is being developed and validated.
- **Effect**: In the dev loop, one error produces N bundles (defeating the cooldown the design relies on to prevent bundle storms), memory grows by an envelope per reload, and the identity semantics the backend queries are silently different from their specification — so the dev-time evidence used to validate the design is not the field's evidence.
- **Owner**: `partial:dispose() doc — HMR is named as a caller but no invariant makes dispose mandatory, idempotent, or self-enforcing, and no rule ties pageIncarnationId to the page rather than the instance`
- **Rationale**: dispose(): "Composition-root lifecycle only (tests, HMR): disarms the nets it armed, frees rings." Q9: "pageIncarnationId (a UUID minted once per page load)" — the two statements are inconsistent the moment dispose/re-create happens, which is precisely what HMR does. The plan-level pass had no design to catch this on.

#### config-and-lifecycle-17 — dispose() vs outstanding RecorderTap handles and app-held unsubscribes

- **Mode** *(medium)*: dispose() defines its effect on nets and rings only, leaving every tap handle and every upstream subscription live and pointed at a dead recorder.
- **Mechanism**: The taps' unsubscribes are held by the composition root, not the recorder — the worked example keeps `stop`, `stopWire2`, and passes `mqttTap` into an onBoundaryDispose closure. dispose() cannot reach any of them. After dispose the wire-1 listener still fires at ~50/s: the app-side callback still allocates `{channel, topic, params, payload}` on every message and still calls `mqttTap.record(...)`. The interface never says what record() on a disposed recorder does — no-op (the app pays allocation forever for nothing), throw (violates I1 at the one moment the recorder is supposed to be gone), or write into freed rings (resurrection of the memory dispose was called to release).
- **Effect**: dispose() does not actually stop the recorder's cost to the app; on the RAM-disk fleet it can also fail to release the envelope if a tap write re-populates a ring, and the semantics are undefined in a way that agent-authored teardown will resolve differently in each call site.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: dispose(): "disarms the nets it armed, frees rings" — 'the nets it armed' is deliberately narrow, and no clause addresses taps. RecorderTap has no 'recorder disposed' state; BucketHealth has detachedAt/detachReason but detach is a caller action, not something dispose is specified to perform. The plan-level pass covered the mirror case (taps dying under the recorder); this is the recorder dying under live taps.

#### config-and-lifecycle-18 — dispose() vs detach() — evidence semantics

- **Mode** *(medium)*: detach() is specified to preserve evidence and dispose() to destroy it, and the design never warns that recreating the recorder is not the same move as re-attaching a tap.
- **Mechanism**: detach(): "Detach never discards evidence: the ring, its records, and the death marker remain for the next bundle — when capture stopped is itself evidence." dispose(): "frees rings." The worked example teaches boundary recovery as detach-then-re-attach, preserving history. An integrator (or an agent) generalizing that pattern one level up — a recovery flow, a feature-flag re-init, an error-boundary reset that rebuilds the composition root — calls dispose() and createFlightRecorder(), which destroys all pre-failure history at the exact moment a failure motivated the recovery, and starts a fresh ring whose first bundle reads as 'nothing happened before the recovery'.
- **Effect**: The single most valuable evidence the recorder holds is deleted by the operation most likely to be reached from a failure path, and the resulting bundle is well-formed and misleading rather than obviously empty.
- **Owner**: `partial:dispose() doc — the destructive semantics are stated but never contrasted with detach's preservation rule, and no invariant forbids or flags a re-create-after-failure pattern`
- **Rationale**: The two contracts sit six lines apart in the same interface with opposite evidence policies and no cross-reference. Q6's "What the judged design leaves deliberately unsolved" list enumerates five carried-forward gaps; the dispose/detach evidence asymmetry is not among them, so it is an unflagged trap rather than an accepted limit.

#### config-and-lifecycle-19 — attach() supersede rule ("a second attach() supersedes the first")

- **Mode** *(high)*: Supersede retires the recorder-side tap but cannot retire the superseded module's upstream subscription, so the boundary keeps dispatching into a dead listener — and which module wins is decided by ESM import order.
- **Mechanism**: attach(): "One live tap per bucket: a second attach() supersedes the first (old tap marked detached 'superseded', epoch++)". The first module's `boundary.actor.on('*', …)` unsubscribe is held by that module, not the recorder. After supersede, that listener still runs on every message inside 0060's shared dispatch turn, building its record literal and calling record() on a superseded tap — whose behaviour is undefined (detach's contract says it "Marks the tap dead in health" and never says record() becomes inert; if it is not inert, the bundle shows records stamped after detachedAt). Two good-faith modules both attaching 'mqtt' — the composition root and a later diagnostics module — is a realistic churn outcome, and the winner is whichever module's side effect runs last in the Vite ESM graph, which reorders when imports are reshuffled.
- **Effect**: Permanent per-message cost in the app's most latency-disciplined path for a listener whose output is discarded; a bucket whose record shape, meta fields, and projection silently change with unrelated import reordering; and a liveness block asserting one attached tap while two listeners run.
- **Owner**: `partial:attach() doc — the supersede rule is deliberate and its recorder-side bookkeeping is specified, but nothing addresses the orphaned upstream subscription, the record()-after-detach semantics, or the nondeterminism of who wins`
- **Rationale**: attach(): "matching source-recreation reality" justifies supersede for the recreate-the-source case, where the old subscription is dead anyway. The two-modules case is a different scenario with the same signature, and I7's "No privileged callers" guarantees both modules can reach it. The design's own worked example demonstrates that the unsubscribe lives outside the recorder (`const stop = boundary.actor.on(...)`).

#### config-and-lifecycle-20 — detach() with a stale tap handle after re-attach (worked example: onBoundaryDispose closure)

- **Mode** *(high)*: detach() is not epoch-guarded, so a stale handle's teardown marks the *live* tap dead in health and in every subsequent bundle.
- **Mechanism**: BucketHealth carries one attachedSince/detachedAt/detachReason/attachEpoch per bucket, not per tap, and detach(reason) takes only a string. The worked example registers `onBoundaryDispose(() => { stop(); stopWire2(); mqttTap.detach('boundary-disposed'); })` capturing that tap in a closure, then teaches recovery as "recreate the boundary, then recorder.attach('mqtt') again". If the old onBoundaryDispose registration is not itself unregistered (nothing in the example does so), the next boundary dispose fires the old closure and calls detach on the epoch-1 handle — while the epoch-2 tap is live and recording. The bucket's health now says detachedAt=T, detachReason='boundary-disposed'.
- **Effect**: The bundle presents the exact three-way liveness diagnosis the design built ("detachedAt set with reason -> died at a known time") with the wrong answer: the reader concludes capture stopped at T and discards everything after, which is the misdiagnosis the liveness block exists to prevent, now produced by the design's own worked example.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: detach(): "Two callers, one member" — the contract enumerates who calls it and never constrains *which epoch* may call it; RecorderTap carries no epoch identity in its signature, and BucketHealth's attachEpoch is a bucket-level counter with no per-tap correspondent. Q6's deliberately-unsolved list names unobservable tap death as the honest limit; a live tap being falsely marked dead is not in that list.

#### config-and-lifecycle-21 — attach epoch vs ring contents and SealedBucket.attach

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Records from multiple attach epochs share one ring under a single attachedSince and no per-record epoch, so the design's claim that re-attach "keeps the gap visible" is not expressible in its own record shape.
- **Mechanism**: The worked example states: "recreate the boundary, then recorder.attach('mqtt') again — epoch++ and a fresh attachedSince keep the gap visible in the next bundle; the old ring's records remain until eviction." But SealedBucket.attach is a single `{attachedSince?, detachedAt?, detachReason?, epoch}` object, and CapturedRecord has fields for seq, both clocks, kind, direction, phase, outcome, correlationId, mime/encoding, bytes, body, markers — and no epoch. So the sealed bucket carries records whose wallMs precedes attachedSince, with nothing marking where epoch 1's records end and epoch 2's begin, and with a single detachReason that either describes epoch 1's death or has been overwritten.
- **Effect**: The bundle is internally inconsistent (records older than the tap that supposedly produced them) and the capture gap the design says is visible is in fact only inferable as a timestamp discontinuity indistinguishable from an idle period; RD-9's oldestIsComplete says nothing about it.
- **Owner**: `partial:I5/RD-9 — the seal copies records and stamps one cut marker per bucket, and RD-9 stamps ring self-sufficiency, but neither carries epoch boundaries into the record stream`
- **Rationale**: SealedBucket's declared fields are exhaustive in the interface artifact; CapturedRecord's likewise. The claim in the worked example is a prose assertion about machinery that the adjacent type definitions do not provide. Q6's deliberately-unsolved list mentions post-trigger records being identified only by `seq > cut.seq` ("a consumer rule, no per-record flag") — the same technique was not extended to epochs, and unlike the cut there is no marker to compare against.

#### config-and-lifecycle-22 — Q11 kill-switch seam (worked example: enabled: startupConfig.flightRecorder !== 'off')

- **Mode** *(high)*: The kill switch fails open: any failure of the startup-config channel, a missing key, or a mis-cased value yields enabled=true — the inverse of the fail-safe posture the same design applies to buckets.
- **Mechanism**: `enabled: startupConfig.flightRecorder !== 'off'` is true for undefined, null, 'OFF', 'false', false, 0, and for a startupConfig object that failed to load and defaulted to {}. The switch exists, per Q11, for "a recorder that is itself degrading the fleet"; the scenarios that make a recorder degrade a fleet (memory pressure, a failing backend, a boot-loop) are correlated with the scenarios that break config delivery, so the switch is likeliest to be inoperative exactly when it is needed. RecorderConfig types `enabled: boolean` but the app's TS is "very loose", so a string or undefined arrives unremarked, and I8's validator posture for non-bucket fields is unstated.
- **Effect**: The one operational lever the design provides for stopping a recorder in the field is unreliable in the direction that keeps the recorder running, and its failure is silent (an enabled recorder looks identical to a correctly-enabled one).
- **Owner**: `partial:Q11 — Q11 rules the seam's shape (one bit, construction-time, ride the startup-config channel, plus a reload) and honestly states the channel presupposition, but never rules the default direction, the coercion, or what the validator does with a non-boolean`
- **Rationale**: Q11: "the seam presupposes the app's startup-config channel exists and carries the bit — a fact to confirm at integration, not a recorder feature." That confirms the channel exists; it does not address what the bit reads as when the channel is present but the value is absent or malformed. RecorderConfig's own comment says "THE kill switch: one bit, read once at construction" and "Disabled: every entry point is an inert no-op" — the disabled branch is fully specified, the fail-to-disabled branch is not.

#### config-and-lifecycle-23 — Q11 kill-switch latency on an always-on fleet

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The switch's effective latency is the tab's lifetime, which on this fleet is unbounded, and the recorder has no whole-instance self-disable to bridge the gap.
- **Mechanism**: Q11: "one `enabled` bit read once at construction ... plus a reload — disable-without-redeploy because redeploy ≠ reload" and "mid-session kill without a reload is unsupported by design". The devices are RAM-disk appliances running one app; nothing in the design or the facts establishes a reload cadence, and there is no in-page mechanism to force one. Meanwhile the design's only self-protective machinery is per-tap: Q11's tapError threshold "detaches the tap (recorded in health as `detached: 'self-protective'`)". There is no equivalent for the recorder as a whole — no self-disable on sustained projectionFailures, on repeated deliveryFailed, on repeated oversized, or on a seal that nearly OOMed.
- **Effect**: If the recorder is the thing degrading a device — a slow project(), a ReDoS pattern, an envelope that is too large for the hardware — the fleet operator's only remedy is a power cycle of every affected device, and the design's stated bias ("a recorder that silently under-captures is acceptable; one that degrades the app is not") has no runtime enforcement above the individual tap.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q11 — the limit is stated but the fleet-specific consequence (unbounded uptime, no forced reload) is not priced, and self-protective behaviour stops at tap granularity`
- **Rationale**: Q11 lists the refused alternatives (recorder-owned remote flag, per-bucket enabled flags) with reasons, and states the limits "rather than hidden". What it does not do is complete the argument for *this* fleet: the panel's rejection of a runtime override seam was justified by "a second config channel startup validation never audits", which is a real cost, but the residual — no way to stop a misbehaving recorder for the remaining uptime of a long-lived tab — is left as prose rather than as a named open item or a self-disable rule.

#### config-and-lifecycle-24 — Composition order: async startup config vs "the recorder is constructed first"

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The design's attach-before-traffic argument assumes synchronous construction, while its own worked example makes the recorder depend on three resolved inputs.
- **Mechanism**: Q4 and attach() both rest on: "Attach order breaks the plan's init cycle: the recorder has no dependencies, so it is constructed first". The worked example contradicts it — construction reads `startupConfig.flightRecorder`, `startupConfig.deviceId`, `BUILD_ID`, and a `postBundleToReportEndpoint` closure. If startupConfig is fetched (the natural reading of "whatever channel already feeds startup config", and the only shape that supports disable-without-redeploy), construction happens after a network round-trip — after React has mounted, after root actors have started, plausibly after the boundary has connected and delivered the `clean:false` redelivery burst. Every tap then attaches late and the boot-window gap the design says it makes visible is the normal case rather than the exception.
- **Effect**: The design's headline resolution of the plan's init-order cycle is conditional on a fact nobody has confirmed; if config is async, the xstate bucket misses initial transitions on every page load, the mqtt bucket misses the reconnect replay burst, and the health block reads 'not yet attached' for a window that the team believes does not exist.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q4 — Q4 specifies attach ordering per tap and stamps attachedSince, but its enabling premise (dependency-free synchronous construction) is asserted rather than checked against the config channel's shape, and no intake item asks whether startup config is sync or fetched`
- **Rationale**: Q4: "the composition root constructs the recorder core first (rings + config, no taps)" — 'rings + config' concedes that config must already exist. Q6's rejected variants list cites "the init-order cycle dissolves by construction order — the recorder has no dependencies, so it is built first" as a reason to refuse the tap-lifecycle registry, so the premise is load-bearing for a design decision, not incidental.

#### config-and-lifecycle-25 — Pre-arm stub (Q7) vs the kill switch and the recorder lifecycle

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The pre-arm stub lives outside the config's blast radius and assumes exactly one drain, so a disabled or re-created recorder leaves it stranded or loses what it captured.
- **Mechanism**: Q7 specifies "a pre-arm stub in the HTML head, before the app bundle: ~30 owned lines that register `error`/`unhandledrejection` listeners into a bounded array ... which the recorder drains and replays into its own nets at init, then removes." Three lifecycle holes follow. (a) If `enabled` is false, "every entry point is an inert no-op" — so nothing drains or removes the stub, whose listeners then stay registered for the page's lifetime, chained ahead of (or behind) whatever incumbent handlers intake item g turns up. (b) If the recorder is disposed and re-created (HMR, a recovery flow), the second instance finds the stub already removed and its replayed records already freed with the first instance's rings — pre-boot evidence destroyed by the re-init. (c) The stub is HTML-head code outside the Vite build and outside the config channel, so it cannot be versioned with the recorder or disabled with it.
- **Effect**: The compensation for the design's acknowledged boot-window gap is the one component the kill switch cannot reach, the dispose contract does not mention, and the config digest cannot attest — and its evidence is silently destroyed by exactly the re-init path the design endorses elsewhere.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q7 specifies the stub's arming and drain but not its interaction with `enabled: false`, with dispose(), or with a second recorder instance. dispose()'s contract ("disarms the nets it armed") is explicitly scoped to nets the recorder armed — the stub's listeners were armed by the HTML, not by the recorder. Nothing in RecorderConfig or RecorderHealth reports whether a stub was found, drained, or left in place.

### Tap adapters and host interactions (25 modes)

#### taps-and-hosts-1 — Q4 tap map — MQTT bucket, wire-1 listener (report.md:84, 466-478)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The projection's cost is bounded by the record it stores, not by the object it walks, so one oversized payload can stall the boundary's dispatch turn and cause the 256-deep shedding the recorder then records.
- **Mechanism**: `maxEntryBytes` (64 KiB in the worked example) truncates the *output* string; the walk that produces it runs to completion first. safe-stable-stringify's only bounds are `maximumDepth`/`maximumBreadth` — the Survey enumerates its full option set and none of them bounds a single string leaf, so a 5 MB string value is fully copied into the serializer's output and UTF-8-metered before truncation copies 64 KiB out of it. 0060 dispatches wire-1 listeners off a bounded delivery queue (`bounds.delivery` default 256, shed-oldest, class-1 `queue-overflow` telemetry, design.md:167, I9 at :488, O1 at :496). Recorder latency in that turn is queue depth growth, then shedding for every consumer.
- **Effect**: The recorder causes message loss for the app it is observing, and the resulting `queue-overflow` telemetry lands in the mqtt bucket as an ordinary record with nothing attributing it to the recorder — the bundle reads as boundary congestion. Violates the stated failure bias (under-capture acceptable, app degradation not).
- **Owner**: `partial:Q11 — names the dispatch-turn budget and defers the number to the spike, but no design element bounds pre-truncation walk cost at runtime`
- **Rationale**: Q11 says "every tap body is bounded — project, meter, push, return; no allocation beyond the record" (report.md:543) and I3 bounds stored bytes. Neither statement is true of the intermediate: the record is bounded, the allocation producing it is not. Q15's spike measures "projection cost on live app-shaped objects" against assumed few-KB payloads (intake item a, unanswered), which does not exercise the single-large-leaf case.

#### taps-and-hosts-2 — Q11 self-interference — tap self-protection

- **Mode** *(high)*: The recorder's only runtime self-protection is throw-counting; a slow-but-correct tap never trips any threshold.
- **Mechanism**: Q11's containment design increments a per-tap `tapError` counter and self-detaches "past a threshold" — the trigger is an exception. A projection that is merely expensive (a payload-size regression on one channel, a getter-heavy context object, a burst) raises no exception and is invisible to that mechanism. Nothing in `RecorderConfig`, `BucketPolicy`, or `RecorderHealth` carries a per-call or per-turn time budget, a moving-average cost, or a degrade-to-shallow path; plan B (capture-turn shallow snapshot) is a design-time fallback chosen by humans after a spike, not a runtime state the recorder can enter.
- **Effect**: The "must never degrade the app" bias is enforced once, before adoption, against assumed payload sizes — and never again in the field. The failure mode is silent and self-reinforcing: cost rises exactly when volume rises.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q11 — throw containment and the kill switch are the only self-protection, and the kill switch needs a reload`
- **Rationale**: Q11 lists throw containment, self-capture cuts, seal budgeting, and the `enabled` bit. The panel explicitly refused any runtime config-override seam (report.md:102), so the only in-field response to a recorder that is degrading the app is a config change plus a page reload — which the report acknowledges ("mid-session kill without a reload is unsupported by design").

#### taps-and-hosts-3 — Q4 xstate bucket / Q3 cost budget

- **Mode** *(high)*: The xstate tap multiplies capture calls per inbound message, but the cost budget is still denominated in the app's 50 msg/s aggregate.
- **Mechanism**: Q4 chooses "snapshot-bearing microstep events plus external events" as the bucket's content (report.md:86). One external event into a machine yields one external-event inspection event plus N microstep events, each carrying a snapshot that references the live machine graph — so the projection serializes the machine's whole context once per microstep. The worked example sizes xstate at `maxCount: 10_000` against mqtt's `5_000` (report.md:453) — an explicit expectation of ≥2× the MQTT record rate — while Q3's budget ("~0.44 µs for a ~200 B record, ~17 µs for ~12 KB … under ~3% of a core at 50 events/s", report.md:78) uses the aggregate message rate as the capture rate.
- **Effect**: The headline cost claim understates total capture work by the microstep multiplier times the context size. If a 12 KB context and 3 microsteps per message are typical, the xstate bucket alone consumes the entire de-rated budget the report allocates to all four buckets.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q3 — the cost model exists but its denominator is the message rate, not the capture rate; Q15's spike inherits the same denominator`
- **Rationale**: Q3's fallback and Q15's spike lane both measure per-record projection cost on "live app-shaped objects"; neither states an event-rate assumption for the xstate tap or requires the spike to measure events-per-message. The report never converts 50 msg/s into a capture-call rate.

#### taps-and-hosts-4 — I7 / FlightRecorder.attach (report.md:243) vs Q4 xstate tap (report.md:86)

- **Mode** *(high)*: "One `actor.system.inspect()` observer per root actor" is unrepresentable on an interface that allows one live tap per bucket.
- **Mechanism**: Q4 prescribes an observer per root actor. `attach(bucket)` documents that "a second attach() supersedes the first (old tap marked detached 'superseded', epoch++)". With N root actors the composition root calls `attach('xstate')` N times: each call marks the previous tap detached. Either the superseded taps keep recording (and `BucketHealth` reports the xstate bucket detached while records arrive on it) or they go inert (and machines 1..N-1 are never captured). The design panel's losing ports variant took `roots: readonly InspectableActor[]` for exactly this reason (design-panel-record.md:1324-1329); the winning surface has no multi-source-per-bucket concept.
- **Effect**: In any app with more than one root actor — routine in xstate codebases — the xstate bucket silently captures one machine's history, or the liveness block lies about which. I7's audit ("if a built-in needs more, this interface is wrong") fails on its own terms and the failure is not noticed.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q4, Q6, the interface, and the worked example all treat a bucket as having exactly one source; the panel record carries the N-roots shape only in a rejected variant, and the judge's recorded rejections (report.md:102) do not mention it. No section reconciles "per root actor" with "one live tap per bucket".

#### taps-and-hosts-5 — I7 / RecorderTap lifecycle (report.md:265-285)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: `detach()`'s effect on subsequent `record()` calls is undefined, so both the supersede path and the boundary-recreation path have two defensible implementations that produce opposite bundles.
- **Mechanism**: detach is documented purely as marking: "Marks the tap dead in health and every later bundle … Detach never discards evidence: the ring, its records, and the death marker remain." Nothing states whether a detached tap's `record()` is inert or still stores. The mqtt worked example detaches on wire-2 'ended' while the wire-1 listener `stop()` is a separate call in a separate callback (report.md:481-484), so a window exists where a detached tap receives records; the supersede path guarantees one.
- **Effect**: Under the inert reading, a boundary that emits 'ended' before its listener is removed silently loses records with no drop counter. Under the live reading, `BucketHealth` reports `detachedAt`/`detachReason` for a bucket that is still filling, so a reader dates the end of capture wrongly. Two implementations of the same interface produce different evidence from the same session.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: The interface comment, Q4's "when taps attach" paragraph, and Q16's liveness reasoning all describe detach as a health marker only. The three-diagnoses table in the worked example (report.md:503-507) reads `detachedAt` as "died at a known time; ring kept" without saying whether the ring is still receiving.

#### taps-and-hosts-6 — I7 / dispose() (report.md:261-262)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Taps outlive `dispose()`, and `record()` on a disposed recorder is undefined behaviour inside someone else's synchronous turn.
- **Mechanism**: `dispose()` "disarms the nets it armed, frees rings" — it does not and cannot unsubscribe the wire-1 listener, the xstate observer, or the 0050 sink, because the composition root owns those subscriptions (Q4's attach map). An xstate inspection event or a wire-1 dispatch arriving after `dispose()` calls `record()` against freed rings. In tests and HMR — the two callers the interface names for dispose — teardown ordering is exactly where this happens.
- **Effect**: I1's "no throw crosses the seam outward" has no stated implementation for the post-dispose state; a null-ring dereference throws into xstate's actor processing loop or the boundary's dispatch turn, i.e. the recorder crashing the app during teardown. At best it is silent no-op capture that tests then read as "the tap works".
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q6's panel refused a tap lifecycle registry, so the recorder has no handle on its taps' subscriptions; `dispose()`'s contract stops at "frees rings" and no invariant covers the disposed state. I1 enumerates projection walks, not lifecycle races.

#### taps-and-hosts-7 — Q4 detach doctrine / Q16 (report.md:481-483, 567)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The MQTT connection's real death state is `degraded`, which triggers no detach — so retry give-up reads in the bundle as "quiet".
- **Mechanism**: 0060's wire-2 states are `idle|connecting|connected|reconnecting|degraded|ended` (design.md:296). `ended` occurs only after `dispose()` (I10 at design.md:489). Bounded retry exhaustion (`maxAttempts` default 10, design.md:163) parks the boundary in `degraded` with `degradedSince` set and publishes gated, indefinitely, until an explicit `reconnect()` (O4 at design.md:499). The worked example's liveness feed tests only `snap.connection === 'ended'`.
- **Effect**: The most common MQTT death — the broker is unreachable and the boundary gave up — produces `attachedSince` present, no `detachedAt`, stale `lastRecordAt`: the report's own "quiet, or an unobservable death, which the reader judges" case (report.md:507). The boundary published a timestamped, machine-readable death and the recorder discarded it.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q16 — claims the misdiagnosis hazard is dissolved by the wire-2 self-detach, on a predicate that fires only after dispose`
- **Rationale**: Q16 states the mqtt tap "self-detaches on wire-2 `connection-ended`" so an empty MQTT bucket "reads as … 'detached at T: connection-ended,' not 'no traffic'". Against 0060's actual state machine that predicate covers dispose only; `degraded` and prolonged `reconnecting` — the states that actually accompany an MQTT incident — leave the bucket in the ambiguous class the section claims to have eliminated.

#### taps-and-hosts-8 — Q4 MQTT bucket vs Q12 replay pre-commitment (report.md:84, 551)

- **Mode** *(high)*: Reconnect message loss is invisible at wire 1, and the gap markers Q12 pre-commits the MQTT record stream to carry cannot be produced by the chosen tap.
- **Mechanism**: 0060 fixes `clean: true` sessions (design.md `BrokerConnectOptions.clean: true`, "A-5: clean sessions, fixed"), so a reconnect drops anything published while disconnected. 0070 makes this explicit: its feed adapter "MUST emit `{ kind: 'gap' }` on any reconnect that may have lost messages — EVERY reconnect under clean:true" (0070 design.md:143,153) and counts `stats.gaps`. Wire 1 carries only `message.*` and `telemetry` (0060 design.md:282); it has no gap concept. The recorder's mqtt bucket therefore splices pre- and post-reconnect records as contiguous history.
- **Effect**: A bundle presents an unbroken message sequence across an interval in which an unknown number of messages were never delivered, with all four drop counters at zero — the well-formed-wrong-evidence class RD-5 exists to prevent, at bucket scope. Q12's commitment that "the MQTT bucket record carries {topic, packetId, payload, direction} plus explicit gap markers in arrival order — the `ValidatedMessage | 'gap'` shape 0070's `RaceHarness.push` consumes" is unbuildable from wire 1, which breaks the stated regression-test path.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q4 chooses wire 1 and never mentions reconnect loss; Q12 asserts gap markers as a design column without naming their source. The recorder already holds a wire-2 subscription that observes every reconnect transition and could stamp a gap record — the design uses that subscription solely to test for 'ended'.

#### taps-and-hosts-9 — Q9 bundle header / Q4 wire-2 use

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Connection-state history never enters the bundle: the recorder reads wire 2 as a trigger and never as a record source.
- **Mechanism**: Q9 puts 0070's six `IngressStats` counters and "wire-2 depths" in the wire body (report.md:525). `BoundarySnapshot` also carries `connection`, `attempt`, `publishGated`, `subscriptions`, and `degradedSince` (0060 design.md:296-301); none is read anywhere in the design. There is no mqtt record kind for connection transitions and no environment field for connection state at seal.
- **Effect**: For any MQTT-shaped incident the first question — was the connection up, how many reconnect attempts, since when degraded, was publishing gated — is unanswerable from the bundle, while the bundle does carry three queue depths. Cross-bucket diagnosis (the recorder's whole claim in Q1) loses its transport-state axis.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q4 assigns wire 2 the single job of feeding `detach`; Q9's environment block enumerates counters and depths only. No section proposes recording connection transitions, and RD-4's direction/outcome reservations do not cover transport state.

#### taps-and-hosts-10 — I6 (report.md:132-136) vs the worked example (report.md:481)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The liveness subscription contradicts I6's absolute wording, costs two snapshot rebuilds per message, and sits one identifier away from a call that changes broker behaviour.
- **Mechanism**: I6 states "The recorder subscribes to nothing"; the worked example calls `boundary.actor.subscribe(...)`. 0060 measured that a live wire-2 subscriber sees two notifications per message because every ingress step (enqueue, dispatch, quarantine push) invalidates the snapshot, each rebuild being O(subscriptions log subscriptions) (design.md:514). Separately, `boundary.subscribe(channel)` on the sibling handle refcounts real broker interest — 0→1 subscribes the filter (design.md:225-230) — and Q4 warns the recorder must never call it, with no lint rule proposed; D-0002's restricted-imports discipline governs modules, not member selection.
- **Effect**: An invariant a reviewer or an agent will read literally is contradicted by the design's own reference wiring, so the rule that actually matters (never take broker interest) loses its force. The rebuild cost is real per-message work that appears in no budget in the report, since Q11 counts only `record()` bodies. A one-token slip converts the passive observer into a subscriber that changes what the broker delivers to the device.
- **Owner**: `partial:I6 — the invariant states the rule but the design's own example violates its wording and no mechanism enforces the part that matters`
- **Rationale**: Q4 states the prohibition in prose ("The recorder must never call `handle.subscribe()`"), which is the right rule, but I6's absolute phrasing plus a contradicting example is exactly the ambiguity loose TS with heavy agent churn resolves badly.

#### taps-and-hosts-11 — Q4 — quarantine ride-along at seal (report.md:84)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The quarantine ride-along re-admits capture-by-reference through a door I2 does not guard.
- **Mechanism**: `quarantine.entries()` returns `readonly QuarantineEntry[]` where `QuarantineEntry = { raw: unknown; error: BoundaryError; endpointOrTopic: string; timestamp: number }` (0060 design.md:315-320) — live boundary and app objects the recorder does not own and did not project. The seal copies them into the bundle; serialization happens later, in the async assembly path (Q7: "Everything after — assembly, redaction …, serialization, optional compression, delivery — is async off the trigger turn", report.md:515). I2 binds only `record()` ("When record() returns, the recorder holds no reference to the caller's entry").
- **Effect**: Between the cut and assembly the recorder holds live references to unvalidated payloads and `BoundaryError` instances — mutable, potentially graph-pinning, and serialized at an unknown later state. This is the exact capture-safety defect the report used to disqualify the Sentry wrap ("breadcrumbs are stored by reference … normalization happens at event-prep time", report.md:39), reintroduced in the recommended build.
- **Owner**: `partial:I2 — scoped to record(); the ride-along enters the bundle without passing record()`
- **Rationale**: I5 governs the seal as "O(n) copy of immutable record references" — true of the recorder's own rings, false of foreign entries that were never projected. No invariant covers content that enters the bundle outside a bucket.

#### taps-and-hosts-12 — Q4 — quarantine ride-along vs I3 (report.md:122-125)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The ride-along section is bounded by count only, and bypasses the byte envelope, the depth/breadth bounds, truncation, and metering.
- **Mechanism**: I3 enforces the 10–50 MB envelope statically as "Σ maxBytes + header allowance ≤ envelope". The ride-along is not a bucket: it has no `maxBytes`, no `maxEntryBytes`, no `project`, and no `schema` row. Its content is 100 entries (0060's ring capacity default) of payloads that by definition failed validation — the population most likely to be oversized, deeply nested, cyclic, or undecodable, since class-4 `undecodable`/`unknown-content-type` bodies land there too (0060 I11 at design.md:490).
- **Effect**: An unbudgeted addition of up to 100 arbitrary payloads to the bundle at the peak-memory instant Q9 spends a paragraph budgeting, and a serialization walk over adversarially-shaped objects with none of the projection's bounds. The static envelope proof in I3 is not a proof of the bundle's size.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q13 — extends redaction to the ride-along; nothing extends metering, bounds, or truncation to it`
- **Rationale**: Q13 names quarantine ride-along as a third seal-time redaction target precisely because "its projection scrubber never ran" (report.md:555). Having identified that the projection never runs on this content, the design applies only the redaction half and leaves the boundedness half — the track's own first constraint — unapplied.

#### taps-and-hosts-13 — Q4/Q9 — quarantine section record discipline

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Quarantine entries cannot carry RD-1/RD-2/RD-3, so the section is un-interleavable with the bucket it is meant to corroborate.
- **Mechanism**: `QuarantineEntry` carries `timestamp: number` (wall ms) and nothing else temporal. The recorder cannot stamp `seq` or a `monoMs` offset from `header.clockOrigin` on events it never observed. Q9 nonetheless says the ride-along is "its own section with the same record discipline" and Q12 claims RD-1/RD-2/RD-3 hold across the design. The ring is also session-scoped and evict-oldest at 100, so its time window bears no fixed relation to the byte-bounded mqtt window — the 100 entries may span the last second or the last four hours, and the section says which only by its own wall timestamps.
- **Effect**: A reader cannot order a reject against the validated messages around it, and any wall-clock adjustment during the session reorders the section relative to the monotonic record stream. The one genuine upside — quarantine can carry pre-`attachedSince` rejects the buckets missed — is neither claimed nor marked.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — asserts "the same record discipline" for a section whose source cannot supply the fields the discipline requires`
- **Rationale**: Q12 lists RD-1/2/3 as "now with their enforcement points named" (ring-assigned at push, shared origin stamped once). The quarantine section has no push and no shared origin; the report does not exempt it or mark it.

#### taps-and-hosts-14 — Q4 MQTT bucket — telemetry half of the wire-1 tap

- **Mode** *(high)*: A validation-failure burst — the archetypal reported incident — appears in the bundle as one telemetry record with no count.
- **Mechanism**: 0060 dedupes telemetry by `dedupKey` over a window (default 60 s): the leading edge emits with `count: 1` and one folded summary emits *at window close* (findings.md check-5: "5 identical rejects → 5 quarantine entries but 1 emission (count: 1), then one summary with count: 5 when the window closes"). A trigger 40 s into a window seals before the summary exists, so the summary rides no bundle. The compensations both undercount: the quarantine ride-along caps at 100, and the recorder's own `DropCounters` are all zero because nothing was evicted, truncated, or failed to project.
- **Effect**: Five thousand failures in the 40 s before the trigger are represented as one record plus 100 quarantine entries, with the true count recoverable from nothing in the bundle. Q1's central justification — that the recorder exists because "telemetry is deduped by design … five identical failures emit twice and the three in between are unrecoverable" — applies verbatim to the recorder's own MQTT error evidence.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q1 — identifies telemetry dedup as the gap the recorder fills, without noticing the chosen tap sits downstream of it`
- **Rationale**: Q4 places the bucket on wire 1, which carries the *deduped* `TelemetryEmission`. Nothing in Q4, Q9, or Q12 marks records as dedup-folded or reconciles the folded `count` against the quarantine section, and no drop species covers upstream folding.

#### taps-and-hosts-15 — Q11 self-capture cut #1 (report.md:545) vs I6 (report.md:132-136)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The `x-recorder-request` marker is inert under I6, and the sentence's literal implementation attaches a non-safelisted header to every app request.
- **Mechanism**: Q11 says "the wrap adds `x-recorder-request: 1` to its POST and filters on it". Under I6 the recorder's POST is a bare fetch behind the transport seam that never reaches the wrapped orval mutator, so the filter can never match — the cut is dead code reading as protection. Read literally, the subject that adds the header is *the wrap*, i.e. the wrapper around `boundary.fetcher`, which sees every app request; `BoundaryFetcher`'s request object does carry `headers?: HeadersInit`, so this is mechanically implementable. `x-recorder-request` is not a CORS-safelisted request header, so any cross-origin partner call gains a preflight. The cited prior art is not the same shape: Sentry's `isSentryRequestUrl` filters on the destination URL, which the recorder already knows.
- **Effect**: Either the stated defence does nothing (and a reviewer believes self-capture is doubly guarded when only I6's structural rule guards it — a rule one line of agent-authored convenience can break by routing the report POST through `customInstance` for auth headers), or the recorder leaks an internal header to partner APIs and adds a preflight round-trip to app traffic: the recorder degrading the app.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q11 — the hazard is owned and the mechanism as written is either inoperative or harmful`
- **Rationale**: I6 makes the structural cut correctly ("the recorder's POST rides a bare fetch behind the transport seam"). Q11 then adds a header mechanism that only makes sense if the POST *does* traverse the wrap, and attributes it to prior art that is URL-based. The two statements cannot both be executed.

#### taps-and-hosts-16 — Q4 HTTP bucket — correlation open-set (RecordMeta.correlationId, report.md:292-296)

- **Mode** *(high)*: Correlation open-set overflow is a loss species the ledger has no counter for, and it silently deletes RD-4's in-flight evidence.
- **Mechanism**: The recorder keeps "a bounded per-bucket open-set: phase 'open' adds the id, 'settled' removes it; ids still open at seal are stamped into the bucket block". `DropCounters` has exactly four species (evicted, truncated, oversized, projectionFailures) — none covers an id evicted from the open-set. The report defers the sizing rule entirely: "the bounded correlation open-set needs a sizing rule before the OpenAPI contract freezes `openAtSeal`" (report.md:104). Under a retry storm or a stalled backend, open ids accumulate precisely when the bound binds.
- **Effect**: `openAtSeal` under-reports in-flight exchanges with every counter reading zero, so I4's "a bundle accounts for every absence; there is no uncounted loss" is false, and the evidence lost is exactly the hanging request Q4's two-record model was introduced to capture.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q6 — the sizing rule is explicitly listed as deliberately unsolved, but I4 asserts a total ledger over it`
- **Rationale**: Q6's "what the judged design leaves deliberately unsolved" names the open-set sizing rule as carried into the enumeration. What it does not carry is the counter: leaving the bound unsized is a schedulable gap; asserting an exhaustive ledger that the bound can violate silently is a defect.

#### taps-and-hosts-17 — Q4 HTTP bucket — record() ordering vs open-set bookkeeping

- **Mode** *(medium)*: If open-set bookkeeping rides the record path, a settled exchange whose outcome record fails to store is reported as in flight at seal.
- **Mechanism**: `record()`'s documented order is "project → bounded-serialize (walk guarded; app code may throw) → redact → meter UTF-8 → truncate-with-copy → stamp → evict-oldest to fit" (report.md:266-272). The open-set update is not placed in that sequence anywhere. If a `phase: 'settled'` entry's projection throws (counted `projectionFailures`) or its truncation floor exceeds `maxBytes` (counted `oversized`), and the open-set removal sits after projection, the id remains open forever.
- **Effect**: `openAtSeal` names a completed request as in flight, and Q5's inference rule — "a request-record with no outcome-record at seal *is* the abort/in-flight evidence" — turns a successful large response into reported evidence of a hang. Well-formed wrong evidence with a nonzero drop counter that nobody would connect to it.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Neither the `RecordMeta` documentation, the `record()` order, nor Q5 states where correlation bookkeeping happens relative to the failure paths. The two placements produce different bundles from the same session and both are consistent with the text.

#### taps-and-hosts-18 — Q4 HTTP bucket — two-record model under evict-oldest

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Ring pressure evicts request-records before their outcomes, so the bucket orphans exactly the half Q5's abort inference depends on.
- **Mechanism**: Eviction within a bucket is evict-oldest (Q2). A request-record is by construction older than its outcome-record, so under pressure the requests go first. The two-record model also doubles the bucket's record rate against a `maxCount: 1_000` (worked example, report.md:452) — 500 exchanges — which a TanStack retry storm (retry lives above the seam, 0060 design.md:250-253, so every attempt is a fresh mutator call and a fresh pair) consumes in seconds.
- **Effect**: The bundle fills with orphan outcome-records whose requests are gone (no URL, no method, no request body) while pre-incident HTTP history is evicted wholesale. `counters.evicted` reports a number but cannot say that half of it was the request side of surviving pairs.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q4 introduces the two-record model to fix the in-flight gap and Q2 fixes evict-oldest as the policy; no section examines their interaction. `openAtSeal` covers only ids still open, not pairs broken by eviction.

#### taps-and-hosts-19 — Q4 HTTP bucket — mutator wrap over BoundaryFetcher.data

- **Mode** *(high)*: Non-plain request bodies serialize to `{}` with no loss marker, or expand into index-keyed objects that blow the walk.
- **Mechanism**: `BoundaryFetcher`'s request is `{ url, method, params?, data?: unknown, headers? }` (0060 design.md:308-311). A `FormData`, `Blob`, or `ReadableStream` in `data` has no enumerable own properties and no `toJSON`, so the bounded serializer emits `{}` — no depth or breadth exhaustion occurs, so the wrapper's elision detector (Q3's "replacer that counts depth/breadth exhaustion") fires no `{elided}` marker. A `Uint8Array`/`ArrayBuffer` goes the other way, expanding to `{"0":…,"1":…}` until `maximumBreadth` truncates it, after a full walk. RD-6's `encoding: 'base64'` is unreachable from this tap: Blob→bytes is asynchronous and `record()` is synchronous fire-and-forget (I9).
- **Effect**: An upload is recorded as an empty body indistinguishable from a genuinely empty one — RD-5's anti-pattern at the record level, produced by the design's own serializer choice — while a binary body imposes an unbounded walk inside the app's request path. RD-6 exists because "binary payloads die without it"; the design reserves the field and provides no way to populate it.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: Q3's projection contract addresses cycles, depth, breadth, getters, and BigInt; the coercion marker cause is listed in `LossMarker` but no mechanism detects the empty-object coercion, because it is not an elision the serializer reports. Q4's HTTP scrubber is specified as "template-key identity, status, and bounded bodies" with no body-type discipline.

#### taps-and-hosts-20 — Q4 log bucket / RecorderTap.detach (report.md:87, 283-284)

- **Mode** *(high)*: The log tap cannot observe its own death; the design kept the claim from the variant whose mechanism the judge rejected.
- **Mechanism**: The interface asserts "the log adapter detaches when the facade drops its sink". 0050's sink surface is `type Sink = (record: LogRecord) => void` with `addSink(id, sink, opts)` and `removeSink(id): boolean` (0050 report.md:128-130) — a bare function with no teardown callback, no disposal signal, and no `hasSink`. Runtime reconfiguration is facade-owned and applied "at boot and on change", and the base's own reporter API is a whole-array replace. The design panel's ports variant supplied this liveness via a `hasSink(id)` probe asked at seal (design-panel-record.md:1332-1340); the judge's recorded rejections include "a tap lifecycle registry with recorder-invoked probes" (report.md:102).
- **Effect**: A config reload that drops the recorder sink leaves the log bucket showing `attachedSince` set, no `detachedAt`, and a stale `lastRecordAt` — read as "quiet". Every bundle after that reconfiguration silently omits logs while asserting the tap is live.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q4 — the log tap is priced for throttle and redaction semantics but its death is claimed, not mechanised`
- **Rationale**: The interface names two self-observing taps (mqtt via wire-2, log via the facade). The first has a cited mechanism; the second's only known mechanism was refused by the same document, and 0050's sketched surface offers no substitute.

#### taps-and-hosts-21 — Q4 log bucket — post-throttle, post-minLevel capture

- **Mode** *(medium)*: Facade-side thinning is invisible to the ledger, and the recorder's own timestamps date sink dispatch rather than emission.
- **Mechanism**: The bucket attaches via `addSink('recorder', sink, { minLevel })` and receives the post-throttle, post-minLevel stream — loss that happens upstream of the recorder's meter, so all four `DropCounters` read zero. 0050 supplies a partial compensation the design does not use: `setThrottleRules` "emits dropped-count meta-events" (0050 report.md:143). Separately, `LogRecord` carries its own `time: number` while the recorder stamps `wallMs`/`monoMs` at `record()` time; with `pauseLogs()/resumeLogs()`-style queueing in the base, dispatch order and dispatch time are not emission order and time.
- **Effect**: I4's "a bundle accounts for every absence; there is no uncounted loss" is false for the log bucket in the ordinary case, and RD-3's single clock domain does not actually hold across it — log records are ordered against MQTT and xstate records by when the facade got round to dispatching them.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q4 — prices throttle thinning as a known 0050 semantic, without reconciling it against I4 or RD-3`
- **Rationale**: Q4 says the bucket "records the post-throttle stream unless the config exempts recorder-relevant patterns", which is an accurate statement of the semantics. It stops short of the consequence: an invariant asserted in absolute terms elsewhere in the same document does not hold for this bucket, and the available reconciliation (dropped-count meta-events) is unused.

#### taps-and-hosts-22 — Q9 environment block — IngressStats read at assembly (report.md:525)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: 0070's counters are read after the cut and cannot be reconciled to it; disclosure via a read clock is not reconciliation.
- **Mechanism**: Q9 routes the six `IngressStats` counters and wire-2 depths through the transport adapter, "read at assembly time and stamped with their own read clock". Assembly follows the synchronous seal, any configured post-trigger window, redaction, and serialization, while ~50 msg/s continues. The counters are cumulative-since-construction monotonic totals, so a later read cannot be back-projected to the cut. A synchronous read at seal costs six integer reads. Additionally `StateKit.stats` is declared as a `readonly` property of type `IngressStats` (0070 design.md:299), not a snapshot function — if it is the live counter object, the bundle holds a mutating reference until serialization and even the read-clock stamp is wrong.
- **Effect**: The header reports `gaps`, `stale`, `unmatched`, and `dispatched` totals that include events the sealed records do not contain — a reader correlating "6 gaps" against the sealed MQTT window finds evidence for two. The recorder's own I5 rationale (index snapshots tear under continued capture) applies to the environment block, where the design does the tearing read deliberately.
- **Owner**: `partial:Q9 — the drift is disclosed via a read clock, not eliminated, and the live-object question is unexamined`
- **Rationale**: Q9 argues the assembly-time read "keeps the recorder observation-only (question 6, I6) and the environment block honest about when it was read". Honest about lateness, yes; but the design refused per-message verdict capture (Q4) *in favour of* these counters, which makes their unreconcilable drift the whole of the recorder's 0070 visibility.

#### taps-and-hosts-23 — Q9 assembly / Q10 delivery — the transport adapter's reach into 0060 and 0070

- **Mode** *(medium)*: A throw in the app-side assembly step loses the whole sealed bundle with no counter and no loss marker.
- **Mechanism**: Assembly is app code ("the transport adapter — app code assembling the OpenAPI wire body from the `SealedBundle`") that reads `kit.stats` and boundary state during the incident being reported — plausibly after `config.signal` aborted the kit or the boundary was disposed. `DropCounters` has no assembly species; `deliveryFailed` counts delivery attempts (Q10). The last-gasp loss marker fires "only when a sealed bundle is in flight or pending" (report.md:527), and a bundle that never finished assembly is arguably neither.
- **Effect**: The trigger returns `{outcome: 'sealed', bundleId}` — a receipt for evidence that then vanishes with the ledger reading clean and the absence-detection backend seeing nothing. The one code path guaranteed to run during a boundary failure is the one with no failure accounting.
- **Owner**: `UNOWNED`
- **Rationale**: I1 covers throws crossing the recorder's seam outward; assembly runs outside the seam by Q9's own design decision. Q10's counters begin at the first delivery attempt, and I4's ledger is scoped to records, not bundles.

#### taps-and-hosts-24 — Q4 — refusal of 0070's `inspect` slot (report.md:84)

- **Mode** *(medium)*: The refusal is argued against a constraint the design's own composition-root doctrine removes, and it costs the message→machine causality chain.
- **Mechanism**: `inspect?: (ev: IngressInspectionEvent) => void` is a `StateKitConfig` field owned by the composition root (0070 design.md:261).
